3 Test-Time Compute

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

When is another token, candidate, check, or model call worth its cost?

An agent that deliberates before acting pays latency and money for a better chance of success, whether it spends them on a longer derivation, more candidate patches, an extra check, or another model call. Each purchase answers a different question. More tokens re-examine what the prompt already holds, more candidates raise the chance that a good one sits somewhere in the pool, and only an executed check adds a fact the model did not have. None of them makes the returned answer correct, because a selector can return a plausible failure, and a search that optimizes against a flawed check finds the flaw faster than the fix. Deliberation is therefore a budgeted allocation problem with a stopping rule. The runtime decides where the next unit of work goes, how far a passing check can be trusted, and when to stop, abstain, or escalate instead of returning its best unverified guess. Every extra turn spent inside one decision lengthens the horizon, so that spending has to be bounded before the agent acts on anything.

Learning Objectives
  • Compare depth, breadth, and feedback as ways to spend a per-decision budget, by the information each adds, the tokens it consumes, and its critical path.
  • Distinguish candidate availability from selected-output quality when evaluating voting, learned scoring, and task-specific checks.
  • Calculate accepted-set precision from stated checker error rates and explain how adaptive search can change those assumptions.
  • Represent a candidate plan as a dependency graph and identify which parts require revision after an observation changes.
  • Analyze how step-level checks and pruning bound a search tree’s growth and its scorer cost.
  • Design stopping rules that enforce resource ceilings without treating budget exhaustion as acceptance evidence.
  • Evaluate competing deliberation policies under a common budget and deadline by pass probability, actual spend, and abstentions.
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

3.1 Limits of a Single Response

The repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) inspects the connection-pool implementation and receives a completed candidate patch. The invocation contract admits it as a candidate, yet the patch rests on an incomplete diagnosis. Client requests intermittently stall even after idle connections become available. Two distinct failure modes can produce this symptom: an aggressive timeout that aborts too early, or a lost notification when a worker thread returns a connection to the pool. When prompted, the language model proposes extending the timeout. That change offers a plausible workaround, but it provides no evidence that the timeout caused the stalls. Deploying such a patch immediately risks masking the latency tail while leaving the concurrency defect intact.

Deliberation extends the horizon with extra model turns and checks inside one decision, and hard ceilings bound that spend.

Before committing to an edit, the agent runtime can spend extra work in three distinct ways. It can give the model a longer output allowance to trace how threads acquire and release the lock step by step. It can sample several candidate patches that modify different parts of the pooling logic. It can also run a targeted test harness to record whether a waiting thread actually receives the condition-variable notification. Each option buys a different kind of evidence. Extended reasoning can expose logical flaws in the source text. Sampling across temperature or prompt variations can surface fixes that a greedy single pass misses. An instrumented test, by contrast, observes runtime state directly and reveals dynamic thread interactions that token generation alone cannot establish.

This division marks the boundary between model computation and runtime observation. Extra model computation re-evaluates information already present in the prompt. Within a single generation, a model can backtrack and revise an earlier analytical misstep even as the previous tokens remain frozen in its prefix. Pure computation, however, cannot create new empirical facts. Only an executed test reports how the running process behaves, and its conclusions hold only for the conditions and thread schedules that the test harness exercised.

Suppose the targeted test forces an interleaving where one worker thread waits while another returns a connection. The trace shows that the connection enters the pool, yet the waiting worker stays blocked past the notification point. That observation rules out the timeout hypothesis immediately and points the agent toward the signaling path. The agent can then write a patch that repairs the notification logic and test it against the failing reproducer. Passing a targeted reproducer still does not establish full task acceptance, because narrow tests can miss edge cases, and neither an unchecked hypothesis nor an isolated micro-test is enough to ship code to production. Even so, the two paths part ways before any file changes (figure 1). Acting on the first guess hides the symptom and keeps the defect, whereas checking first spends one short test to learn which hypothesis survives.

Napkin Math 3.1: A probe vs. a longer answer
Problem: A prepared diagnostic test takes 40 ms. A proposed explanation contains 1,024 output tokens. How long does the explanation take to generate relative to the test?

Variables: The illustrative per-token generation time from Estimating per-call hardware work is 21.07 ms, derived from memory-bandwidth limits rather than a measured production service rate. The hypothetical output pricing is $15 per million tokens. Prompt processing, queueing, transport, test preparation, and the downstream model call that reads the test observation are excluded.

Math: Generating 1,024 tokens at 21.07 ms per token requires 21.58 s and incurs $0.01536 in token charges. Dividing this generation latency by the 40 ms test run yields a factor of 539.5×.

Systems insight: When the unresolved question concerns dynamic program behavior, the probe settles it 539.5× faster than the model can generate a speculative explanation of it, and only the probe’s answer is an observation. Running a test is preferable whenever its preparation and execution time remain below the explanation’s 21.58 s and its outcome cleanly separates the competing failure modes. Conversely, an inconclusive test that does not separate the hypotheses provides no diagnostic gain regardless of its speed. Failure to reproduce can still supply negative evidence when the test’s sensitivity to the suspected fault is known. A sound runtime spends work on whichever operation most cheaply removes the main source of uncertainty.

The probe, and the decision to revise the patch on its evidence, both happened outside any single model call. The invocation contract of the previous chapter ends at one prompt and one candidate, accompanied only by metadata indicating whether generation reached a stop token or an output limit. That interface cannot decide whether the next second of latency or fraction of a cent is better spent on longer reasoning, extra candidate samples, or a runtime probe. An API response marked complete confirms only that generation finished, not that the proposed patch fixes the bug. Test-time compute is all the system work the runtime orchestrates around the model call, with model weights held fixed, to generate, test, and select candidates before committing to an action. Governing that spend requires a runtime policy that sets hard ceilings on latency and cost, routes work across reasoning and checks, and stops cleanly without mistaking an exhausted budget for a verified solution.

↩︎ The bounded single-invocation model contract is established in The Invocation Contract.

Each part of that policy raises the next question. The runtime first chooses how to spend extra work across reasoning depth, candidate breadth, and empirical feedback (section 3.2). Breadth produces a pool, so the runtime must then pick from it without being fooled by a plausible failure (section 3.3), and picking well depends on how far a passing check can be trusted (section 3.4). When a check refutes a premise partway through a plan, the runtime should regenerate only what depended on it (section 3.5). Candidates and revisions together form a search whose spending must be tracked, reserved, and stopped (section 3.6), and the complete policy is finally judged against its alternatives under one budget and one deadline (section 3.7).

3.2 Three Axes of Test-Time Compute

Giving a repair agent a four-thousand-token allowance does not determine its latency, memory footprint, or diagnostic reach, because the runtime can spend those same four thousand tokens on one long derivation, four parallel candidate patches, or three edit–test–revise rounds. Although all three options can consume the same number of output tokens, they stress the serving system in different ways. A long derivation generates tokens one after another along a single causal chain. Independent candidate patches can run concurrently across accelerator hardware. An edit–test–revise loop must pause model generation while each diagnostic test runs. Those dependencies determine which operations sit on the critical latency path and how much state the serving engine must hold in memory.

Depth extends sequential computation within a single candidate, breadth explores multiple candidate paths concurrently, and feedback folds environment observations into subsequent revisions. A test-time deliberation rollout is one bounded candidate-generation path, potentially interleaving verification checks and observation-driven revisions; the term comes from game search, where a rollout plays one line of moves forward to its end. Depth lengthens a rollout, breadth runs several in parallel, and feedback inserts checks between a rollout’s turns. An allocation policy can combine all three: it might generate several candidate patches in parallel, run regression tests against each, and spend deeper step-by-step reasoning only on the most promising survivor. Throughout, model weights remain frozen, and every axis draws from the same test-time budget.

Definition 3.1: Test-time compute budget

Test-time compute budget is the per-decision allowance of processed prompt tokens, generated tokens, model invocations, verification checks, and elapsed wall-clock time that a runtime may expend, with model weights fixed, before it must select a candidate, abstain, or escalate to a human operator.

  1. Significance: The budget sets the attempt cost in the effective cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask = \mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\) (equation 1.2). Additional deliberation reduces cost per accepted task when it increases the acceptance rate \(\AcceptanceProbability\) proportionally faster than it increases the expected attempt cost. A higher cost per accepted task can still be justified when it buys the required acceptance rate within the deadline (section 3.7.3).
  2. Distinction: Training compute amortizes weight optimization once across all future tasks. Test-time compute recurs on every decision, where an internal reasoning allowance is merely one control surface alongside external tool invocations and verification checks.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the maximum allocation ceiling as the expected spend, or treating an exhausted budget as evidence that the best remaining candidate is acceptable.

What separates the three axes is their dependency structure (figure 2). Depth extends a single candidate through multi-step reasoning, breadth fans out to sampled candidates that converge on a selector, and feedback routes a candidate through a sandboxed check whose observation conditions the revision.

3.2.1 Extending reasoning along the depth axis

Asking a model to jump straight from a bug report to a patch often misses multi-step lock interactions, yet letting it reason for thousands of tokens puts every step onto the critical latency path and can exhaust the output limit before any code is emitted. Extending a call along the depth axis lets an autoregressive model unroll intermediate reasoning tokens before emitting its final decision or artifact, generalizing early scratchpad techniques and chain-of-thought prompting (Nye et al. 2021; Wei et al. 2022). In the connection-pool scenario, these intermediate tokens might trace thread interleavings step by step: acquiring the pool mutex, checking for leaked socket descriptors, and signaling waiter condition variables. Because autoregressive generation conditions on all prior tokens, later decoding steps can inspect earlier derivations, catch flawed assumptions, and change course before emitting code. That gain comes entirely from running extra forward passes over the growing context rather than changing the network weights.

Nye, Maxwell, Anders Johan Andreassen, Guy Gur-Ari, Michal Michalewski, Jacob Austin, David Bieber, David Dohan, et al. 2021. “Show Your Work: Scratchpads for Intermediate Computation with Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2112.00114, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2112.00114.

On the hardware, however, extending a single generation path serializes the work. Because transformer decoding at small batch sizes is memory-bandwidth bound, step latency is set by the time needed to stream all model parameters from high-bandwidth memory into processor caches (principle 2). Wall-clock generation time therefore scales roughly linearly with the number of generated tokens under a fixed serving setup. A long reasoning trace shows only that the accelerator performed more work, not that the resulting code compiles or runs correctly. The runtime must also leave enough output budget for the final patch: a derivation that hits the token limit mid-thought returns a truncated response, which the invocation contract quarantines like any other incomplete call rather than admitting as a candidate.

3.2.2 Parallel sampling along the breadth axis

Drawing a single completion commits the repair to whatever hypothesis the model’s first tokens favored, and if that initial framing is wrong, extra depth only elaborates a flawed premise. Sampling along the breadth axis hedges against that commitment by requesting multiple candidate patches from the same source excerpt and task specification using stochastic decoding controls. Conditional on a fixed prompt and independent random seeds, these samples are statistically independent draws from the model’s output distribution. Even so, they remain bounded by what the model knows: if the model assigns negligible probability mass to a valid thread-notification pattern, every independent sample can repeat the same missing broadcast. Independent draws can still fail in identical ways.

↩︎ Temperature, top-\(\Glyph{k}\), and nucleus sampling controls are established in Sampling controls.

Because parallel candidates do not depend on one another, breadth exposes fine-grained concurrency at modest latency cost. Under memory-bandwidth-bound decoding (principle 2), a batch of \(\CandidatePoolSize\) concurrent candidate streams reads the model weights once per step for all \(\CandidatePoolSize\) tokens. Spending an allowance of \(\CandidatePoolSize \times \OutputLength\) tokens sequentially along the depth axis multiplies generation latency by roughly \(\CandidatePoolSize\). Generating \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidates of length \(\OutputLength\) in parallel, by contrast, keeps total generation time close to the duration of the longest candidate, \(\max_n \DecodeInterval(\OutputLength_n)\), provided the batch stays memory-bandwidth bound and fits in accelerator memory.

Accelerator memory is where breadth extracts its price. Each active generation stream must maintain its own key-value (KV) cache for the tokens it generates. When the serving system shares prefixes across streams, the \(\SequenceLength\)-token prompt is stored once in memory, and peak KV cache demand scales as

\[ \BreadthPeakKvCacheMemory \propto \SequenceLength + \sum_{n=1}^{\CandidatePoolSize} \OutputLength_n \]

Without shared prefix caching, the serving engine stores \(\CandidatePoolSize\) copies of the prompt’s KV tensors, and memory demand scales as \(\CandidatePoolSize \SequenceLength + \sum_n \OutputLength_n\). When total KV cache demand exceeds hardware capacity, the serving engine must split candidates into sequential waves. Once forced into waves, breadth latency degrades from the duration of the single longest candidate toward the sum across waves, while memory thrashing and queueing delays stretch individual token latencies. Whether identical prompt prefixes share physical memory or receive provider billing discounts depends on the serving engine.

↪ Block sharing, which stores a common prompt’s KV state once for every candidate that branches from it, is developed in Copy-on-write prefix sharing.

Breadth also shifts the burden downstream, because generating a wide pool of candidates pays off only if the runtime can tell which candidate is best. A pool that holds a working patch is useless if the selector cannot identify it. In a study of repeated sampling, Brown et al. (2024) showed that the probability that at least one candidate is correct (pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\)) kept rising across four orders of magnitude of samples. In contrast, practical selectors such as majority voting and learned reward models (scorers trained to predict whether a candidate is correct, examined in section 3.4.1) plateaued after a few hundred samples. Candidate selection is therefore a major consumer of compute and verification budget, not an incidental final step.

3.2.3 Revising from observations along the feedback axis

Neither a longer chain of thought nor a wider pool of samples tells the model whether a waiting thread actually receives a condition-variable signal at runtime. The feedback axis supplies that missing evidence by executing a check on a candidate and feeding the resulting observation into the next model request. Instead of relying on frozen weights and a static prompt alone, the repair agent proposes a candidate patch, the runtime runs a bounded test suite, and the next model call receives concrete diagnostics such as a stack trace from a deadlocked worker thread. Those runtime observations narrow the next candidate far more sharply than asking the model to reread the original prompt on its own (Shinn et al. 2023).

Shinn, Noah, Federico Cassano, Ashwin Gopinath, Karthik Narasimhan, and Shunyu Yao. 2023. “Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 8634–52.

Verification checks return structured outcomes: failure, pass, or inconclusive execution (such as a test timeout). A failure trace points to broken assertions or regressions; a passing test confirms that the code satisfies the exercised assertions under the tested conditions; and final acceptance remains a separate policy gate. Running diagnostics is itself an external action that consumes CPU and memory, runs inside a sandbox, and adds wall-clock latency even when used only inside deliberation.

Each feedback round is also one more turn of the horizon \(\TurnHorizon\), which ties this axis to horizon compounding (equation 1.1). Unchecked turns compound error as \((1-\PerStepErrorRate)^{\TurnHorizon}\), and the introduction showed (Reliability and Cost over a Horizon) that a check which rejects a bad step and retries it resets that compounding. Feedback is the one axis that places such a check inside every turn, so it can lengthen a deliberation without the unchecked compounding its extra turns would otherwise carry, to the extent that its checks deserve trust (section 3.4).

Feedback forces strict serialization because the next revision cannot start until the current check finishes and returns its observation. Context policies that append every prior candidate and diagnostic report also make the input prompt grow from turn to turn. For an initial prompt length \(\BasePromptLength\), candidate lengths \(\OutputLength_j\), and observation lengths \(\lvert \TrajectoryObservation_j\rvert\), an append-only policy yields an input length at turn \(\TurnIndex\) of

\[\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)} = \BasePromptLength + \sum_{j=1}^{\TurnIndex-1}(\OutputLength_j + \lvert \TrajectoryObservation_j\rvert)\]

This is the append-only growth that equation 2.9 priced across a whole trajectory, now confined to one decision, and it drives up prompt prefill latency, accelerator memory usage, and token billing on every round. Context-compaction methods can summarize earlier attempts or drop stale diagnostic traces, but the allocation policy must budget for the full token volume actually sent to the model API.

↪ Context-compaction methods and their scheduling within the request budget are developed in Context Compaction.

3.2.4 The three axes under one output ceiling

Capping generated tokens at a single number does not cap wall-clock latency, accelerator memory, or input-token billing, because depth, breadth, and feedback turn the same output ceiling into different resource loads. Runtimes therefore track four quantities across the three axes: input and output token counts, critical-path wall-clock latency, peak retained accelerator state, and external information gained. Let \(\PrefillTime(\SequenceLength)\) be the time required to ingest an \(\SequenceLength\)-token prompt, \(\PerTokenDecodeTime\) the per-token decoding step time, and \(\DecodeInterval(\OutputLength)\) the total generation time for \(\OutputLength\) tokens. Let \(\FeedbackCheckDuration\) be the duration of the external check in feedback round \(\TurnIndex\), which is also the turn index of the trajectory. With \(\ExtendedDepthLength\) tokens of extended depth, \(\CandidatePoolSize\) breadth candidates executed concurrently, and \(\TurnHorizon\) feedback turns, the latency dimension decomposes into three critical paths:

\[ \begin{aligned} \DepthCriticalPathLatency &\approx \PrefillTime(\SequenceLength) + \ExtendedDepthLength\,\PerTokenDecodeTime,\\ \BreadthCriticalPathLatency &\approx \PrefillTime(\SequenceLength) + \max_{n} \DecodeInterval(\OutputLength_n),\\ \FeedbackCriticalPathLatency &\approx \sum_{t=1}^{\TurnHorizon}\bigl(\PrefillTime(\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)}) + \DecodeInterval(\OutputLength_t) + {\FeedbackCheckDuration}_{\TurnIndex}\bigr). \end{aligned} \tag{1}\]

The critical path for breadth assumes the serving cluster has enough memory and compute capacity to decode all \(\CandidatePoolSize\) streams concurrently at an unchanged step time. When capacity saturates, decoding falls back into serialized waves and shifts the critical path toward the sum across candidate streams. The feedback expression, by contrast, grows from two sources: sequential check durations \(\FeedbackCheckDuration\) and lengthening prompt prefill times \(\PrefillTime(\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)})\) as context accumulates. Equation 1 covers only latency; table 1 sets those critical paths beside the tokens processed, the peak model state retained, and the external information acquired.

Of the three axes, only feedback brings in external information absent from the model weights and prompt. The calculation below evaluates these expressions for the connection-pool repair under a fixed output ceiling of 4,096 tokens, showing how equal output ceilings produce different latencies and token bills.

Napkin Math 3.2: One decision, three allocations
Problem: A repair request starts with 4,096 input tokens and a common ceiling of 4,096 generated tokens. How do depth, breadth, and feedback spend that allowance?

Variables: Reuse the illustrative per-token generation time from the preceding example. Breadth has capacity for 4 concurrent streams at that same rate. Each short candidate uses 1,024 generated tokens. Feedback uses 3 turns, each followed by a 1.5 s check returning 340 tokens. Later feedback requests append earlier candidates and reports. Hypothetical prices are $3 per million input tokens and $15 per million output tokens, without cache discounts, so each breadth stream is billed for its own copy of the prompt (unlike the shared-prefix row of table 1). Generation times omit prefill, queueing, and transport; the feedback time additionally includes its checks. The bill counts model tokens only, and table 2 tallies each allocation’s tokens, time, and bill side by side.

Math: In table 2, depth generates all 4,096 tokens along a single path. Breadth generates 1,024 tokens on each of 4 concurrent paths, shortening the critical path by 4×. However, reading the prompt 4 times across unshared streams adds 12,288 input tokens, multiplying its billing cost to 1.5× that of depth. Feedback accumulates input context over \(\TurnHorizon\) turns according to \(\TurnHorizon\,\BasePromptLength + (\OutputLength + \lvert \TrajectoryObservation\rvert)\,\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon-1)/2\), reaching 16,380 tokens at \(\TurnHorizon =\) 3 because each successive round re-reads all prior candidates and observation reports.

Systems insight: Breadth trades money for lower wall-clock latency, and its entire billing premium comes from the \((\CandidatePoolSize-1)\SequenceLength\) re-read prompt tokens. The governing ratio is prompt length relative to generated output: a large repository context makes breadth’s input bill grow steeply with both \(\CandidatePoolSize\) and \(\SequenceLength\), whereas a short prompt or a shared prefix cache removes most of that surcharge. Feedback lands between depth and breadth in both latency and billing while remaining the only axis that acquires new runtime observations. Because this accounting leaves out pass rates, it prices the work rather than the quality of the result.

3.2.5 Internal budgets and external orchestration

When a model service hides its intermediate reasoning tokens behind an API, a runtime that gauges effort from the length of the visible reply understates both token spend and latency. Production systems therefore separate two control surfaces: internal model deliberation and external workflow orchestration. A model service may expose an internal budget that caps how many intermediate tokens a single call may spend, while the runtime independently controls how many calls to dispatch, which tool checks to run, and what context to pass into subsequent requests. Internal budgets adjust depth inside one call; external orchestration governs breadth across branches, schedules sandboxed checks, and assembles revised prompts. Because the service may redact intermediate tokens from the returned text, the runtime must read explicit token-usage headers and termination status codes rather than character counts to enforce quotas and track spend.

3.2.6 How reasoning models spend output allowances

That internal budget governs reasoning models, which can otherwise burn an entire output allowance on intermediate steps and return an empty or truncated final answer. Trained with reinforcement learning on tasks with objective, verifiable outcomes, such as formal mathematics, logic puzzles, and unit-tested codebases, these models learn to generate extended intermediate scratchpads that propose solutions, check steps, and backtrack within a single call (OpenAI 2024; DeepSeek-AI et al. 2025). At the serving layer, those intermediate reasoning tokens are ordinary autoregressive output tokens that consume accelerator compute, fill the KV cache, and incur full output-token charges. Because that scratchpad draws from the call’s output allowance, services expose a reasoning-budget parameter that turns sequential depth from a fixed model habit into a per-request runtime knob. The orchestrator can set that knob by task, yet internal reasoning still operates strictly over the supplied prompt and static weights. An extended scratchpad can check arithmetic or trace logic across the prompt, but it cannot observe runtime behavior or tell whether a proposed patch fixes a connection leak without running a test suite.

OpenAI. 2024. Learning to Reason with LLMs. OpenAI Research Blog.

↪ Reinforcement learning on tasks with verifiable outcomes, the training that produces reasoning models, is developed in Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards.

Because models trained with verifiable rewards spend output allowances differently than instruction-tuned models (models adapted to follow example instructions and responses), token ceilings must be calibrated per model family, and call telemetry guides orchestration policy without guaranteeing that a completed candidate is correct. Treating depth as a per-call knob alongside external breadth and feedback still lets the runtime vary spend across tasks, raising the practical question of which allocation axis pays off at each level of task difficulty.

Example 3.1: Compute-optimal test-time allocation
Scenario: Snell et al. (2024) evaluated strategies for spending a fixed test-time compute budget across reasoning benchmarks, comparing sequential revision of a single candidate against parallel breadth sampling and search guided by learned process verifiers (section 3.4). The study grouped benchmark problems into difficulty tiers by the base model’s single-shot success rate.

Diagnosis: On easier problems, sequential revision used the budget most effectively, fixing minor mistakes without the overhead of parallel sampling. On moderately difficult problems, hybrid strategies combining parallel exploration with verifier-guided search reached higher accuracy. On the hardest problems, scaling test-time compute hit diminishing returns across all axes. At matched total FLOPs, a well-chosen test-time allocation let a smaller model beat a model 14 times larger whenever the smaller model had a nonzero base success rate. When base success sat near zero, spending FLOPs on a larger base model beat exhausting tokens on parallel rollouts.

Systems lesson: With the supplied prompt held fixed, extra reasoning and sampling can surface capabilities the model already has, but they cannot supply missing runtime facts. Feedback can bring new observations into the next call, though their value still depends on the check’s coverage and the model’s ability to use them. Runtimes should route budgets by estimated difficulty: spend a small budget or a single call on straightforward tasks, use parallel search and feedback on moderately hard tasks, and escalate near-zero-yield requests to a stronger model or a human operator instead of burning tokens on unguided sampling.

Because a runtime does not know a task’s true difficulty in advance, it estimates difficulty with cheap, fast signals before committing a large deliberation budget. Common signals include agreement among two or three short pilot samples, fast static linters, and historical failure rates on similar requests. When pilot samples agree and pass static checks, the runtime keeps the budget small and finishes in one call. When pilot samples disagree or linters flag errors, the scheduler escalates to broader sampling or an edit–test–revise loop, and if the budget ceiling is reached without a verified candidate, it stops and escalates to a human operator. Each routing signal acts as a classifier whose precision and recall should be measured on held-out tasks.

In practice, internal depth and external breadth work together. Each rollout may use enough depth to draft a coherent code patch while the runtime samples several such patches in parallel and filters them through a test harness. Even so, the mix has to fit the system’s bottlenecks: slow tests stretch the feedback critical path, and weak tests dull the value of each observation. Those same checks also matter during training, because reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards uses them as a training reward. Any blind spot or false pass that causes a bad selection at test time therefore becomes a loophole that training actively optimizes for on every weight update.

↪ How an acceptance check becomes a training reward, and how its gaps are exploited during training, is developed in Verifiable Rewards.

Checkpoint 3.1: Choosing an allocation

Choosing how to spend compute across depth, breadth, and feedback determines what candidates enter the pool, but generating a good candidate does not help if the selector picks a plausible broken one instead. The next step is choosing among candidates without spending more on selection than the pool is worth.

3.3 Selecting from a Candidate Pool

Sampling eight candidate patches for the connection pool helps nothing if seven of them repeat the same broken timeout workaround and the selector picks one of the seven over the single patch that fixes the notification bug. In a typical pool, one candidate might lengthen an acquisition timeout, another might add exponential retries, and a third might change how the connection-return path wakes waiting threads. The runtime has to decide which proposal, if any, has enough supporting evidence to return, and gathering that evidence consumes time and tokens from the same deliberation budget that produced the pool.

Two questions separate generation from selection: whether the pool holds at least one candidate that solves the task, and whether the selector can pick that candidate without accepting a broken one. A generator can flood the pool with proposals without making selection any easier; conversely, a sharper selector can raise end-to-end reliability without sampling a single extra candidate.

3.3.1 Useful variation among candidates

Renaming local variables, reformatting comments, or rewriting a loop can produce eight distinct token sequences that all leave the synchronization bug untouched. Surface variation in token sequences does not guarantee diverse repair hypotheses. In the connection-pool repair, candidates add value only when they test different explanations that downstream checks can tell apart. Changing the thread-notification path vs. lengthening the timeout tests two distinct hypotheses. Generating half a dozen slightly reworded versions of the same timeout bump only wastes verification runs.

This problem is separate from statistical dependence. When the task, prompt, base model, and sampling controls stay fixed, separate draws with independent random seeds are genuinely independent and identically distributed (IID). Even so, if the model puts almost all of its probability mass on timeout edits, independent draws will still fill the pool with variations on that one mistake.

Across a mixed production workload, shared model blind spots matter even more. A model often solves familiar patterns reliably while failing every time on an unfamiliar edge case, so repeated samples on a hard task fail together far more often than the average pass rate across all tasks would suggest. While samples on a single fixed task may be conditionally independent, treating attempts across a mixed workload as draws with one shared pass probability hides that clustering. Feedback-driven revision changes the distribution again: once the runtime feeds test errors into later prompts, each new candidate depends directly on what earlier checks found.

A runtime can encourage broader exploration by raising sampling temperature or prompting the model to try distinct diagnostic angles under the same task constraints. Pushing diversity too hard, however, often produces syntax errors, broken invariants, or nonsensical edits. A diversification method earns its keep only if it raises the task pass rate within a fixed compute and latency budget.

To avoid wasting test runs on near-duplicates, the runtime can prune redundant candidates early by deduplicating on structure and behavior. Abstract syntax tree (AST) normalization strips away surface changes such as variable names and whitespace, and behavioral clustering runs candidates on cheap inputs to group those with identical outputs. Using both steps let a competitive code-generation system narrow tens of thousands of raw model samples down to a handful of distinct submissions (Li et al. 2022). Each method groups candidates by a bounded equivalence relation, whether normalized syntax or matching outputs on a probe set, and deduplication is sound whenever that relation preserves the property the downstream check tests.

Li, Yujia, David Choi, Junyoung Chung, Nate Kushman, Julian Schrittwieser, Rémi Leblond, Tom Eccles, et al. 2022. “Competition-Level Code Generation with AlphaCode.” Science 378 (6624): 1092–97. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.abq1158.

3.3.2 Choosing a selection rule

Picking the most common patch by majority vote fails whenever the model’s favored mistake outnumbers the one candidate that fixes the bug, whereas running the full test suite on every raw sample wastes sandbox time on patches that do not even parse. Runtimes therefore combine three kinds of selection signal, ordered by cost and by the strength of the claim each supports: direct execution checks, answer agreement (consensus voting), and learned ranking.

Direct checks, such as a parser, a type checker, or a test harness, test a candidate deterministically against explicit rules. Each establishes only the property it checks, as the scoped check pipeline of Staging checks by cost and scope showed, so clean compilation says nothing about whether a race condition is gone. Running candidate code is also an external action that requires sandbox isolation, hard timeouts, and tight permission boundaries.

Answer agreement combines independent reasoning paths by vote. In self-consistency, the runtime samples several reasoning chains, normalizes their final answers, and takes the plurality vote (Wang et al. 2023). That works well for arithmetic, symbolic logic, or short factual answers, but majority vote still reflects only what the generator produces most often. When a model shares a systematic bias or takes a flawed shortcut, independent samples regularly converge on the same wrong answer. For code repair, voting over raw patches is a poor selector: behavioral agreement inherits the coverage limits of the probe inputs, and a minority patch that actually passes the reproducer should beat a popular patch that fails it.

When deterministic checks cannot cover every requirement, learned rankers and model-based judges supply a heuristic ordering. A learned reward model or a secondary large language model (LLM) judge (a model prompted to grade another model’s output) can score explanation clarity, check style guidelines, or rank patches before the runtime spends time on heavy integration tests. Even so, ranking and acceptance answer different questions. Ranking orders candidates from best to worst under a score; acceptance decides whether a candidate meets the task contract. If every candidate in the pool is broken, picking the highest-ranked candidate returns the best-looking failure. Returning a candidate instead of abstaining requires a verifier that produces an objective pass or fail result.

Definition 3.2: Verifier-guided selection

Verifier-guided selection is a selection policy that returns a candidate only when a verifier, a check whose criteria and observations the candidate cannot modify, passes it, and abstains when no candidate passes.

  1. Significance: Its success rate is capped by pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\), the chance the pool holds a passing candidate (section 3.3.3), while the share of its selections that are correct depends on both the verifier’s passing-set precision and the selection policy (section 3.4.2). The fixed IID first-passing policy developed there preserves passing-set precision; ranking among passing candidates need not. Selection coverage and selected correctness together determine the probability of returning a valid result.
  2. Distinction: Voting selects what the generator produces most often and learned ranking selects what a scorer prefers; only a verifier’s verdict can justify returning a candidate instead of abstaining.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the verifier’s pass as task acceptance; the verdict covers the checked property, and its false acceptance rate grows in importance as the pool’s valid-candidate rate falls.

The hypothetical repair pool in table 3 shows what each check stage can and cannot establish. Here a focused reproducer exercises the known race condition, while full regression suites have not yet run.

A production runtime stages these checks cheapest first, following the cost-ordered cascade of Scoped candidate checks, so that parsing and AST checks drop malformed candidates before they reach sandboxed tests or an LLM judge. Inside a search, the stage order should come from measured run time and rejection rate rather than guesswork. Running a compiled binary in a warm container might take 5 ms, whereas calling a large LLM judge to grade code quality is a full model call that can take 800 ms and eat into the token budget. Comparing several candidates in a single prompt also scales differently with context length than scoring each candidate on its own. Because checking competes directly with generation for time and tokens, selection has to be budgeted as part of test-time compute.

3.3.3 Candidate availability under pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\)

When a runtime fails to return a working patch from a pool of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) samples, the engineer needs to know whether the generator never produced a passing patch or the selector threw a passing patch away. pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) separates those two failures by measuring the probability that at least one of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) sampled candidates passes the specified tests (Chen et al. 2021).1

1 Origin of pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\): The metric was introduced to score program synthesis by functional correctness rather than by textual similarity to a reference (Kulal et al. 2019). Because plugging the observed pass fraction \(\PassingSampleCount/\GeneratedSampleCount\) into \(1-(1-\PassingSampleCount/\GeneratedSampleCount)^\IndependentAttemptCount\) biases the estimate of what breadth buys, Chen et al. (2021) replaced it with the combinatorial estimator derived below.

Kulal, Sumith, Panupong Pasupat, Kartik Chandra, Mina Lee, Oded Padon, Alex Aiken, and Percy S. Liang. 2019. “SPoC: Search-Based Pseudocode to Code.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 32.
Chen, Mark, Jerry Tworek, Heewoo Jun, Qiming Yuan, Henrique Ponde de Oliveira Pinto, Jared Kaplan, Harri Edwards, et al. 2021. “Evaluating Large Language Models Trained on Code.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2107.03374.

For a fixed task and fixed generation policy, suppose independent samples each pass the tests with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\). The probability that all \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) samples fail is \((1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\), so

\[ \operatorname{pass@}\IndependentAttemptCount = 1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount. \]

This expression is conditional on that task and sampling policy. To summarize a workload with different task-level pass probabilities, average the task-level quantities; substituting the workload’s average \(\AttemptPassProbability\) generally gives a different result. For an adaptive policy that revises its prompt after feedback, evaluate the policy’s resulting pools rather than assuming they follow this fixed-distribution formula.

In a benchmark, generate \(\GeneratedSampleCount \ge \IndependentAttemptCount\) candidates for a task and let \(\PassingSampleCount\) pass the fixed test criterion. Of the \(\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}\) subsets of size \(\IndependentAttemptCount\), exactly \(\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount-\PassingSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}\) contain only failures. The subset estimator is therefore

\[ \PassAtKEstimate = 1-\frac{\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount-\PassingSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}}{\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}}, \]

with the numerator taken as zero when \(\GeneratedSampleCount-\PassingSampleCount<\IndependentAttemptCount\). Under independent, identically distributed sampling for the fixed task, this estimates the probability that a fresh pool of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) candidates contains a test-passing member without bias; Pass@\IndependentAttemptCount and repeated-run consistency derives this expectation and details evaluation without factorials. Benchmark results average these estimates over tasks (Chen et al. 2021).

The relationship to deployed selection follows from the event being measured. If the selector must return an unchanged member of the same pool, it cannot return a test-passing candidate when the pool contains none. Its probability of returning a test-passing candidate is therefore at most pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\), counting abstention as no returned pass. A selector that always finds a passing member whenever one exists reaches this ceiling; whether the deployed selector does, within the deployment’s checking budget, is a separate measurement.

The predicate in pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) is passing these tests, which is narrower than satisfying the complete task requirements. The ceiling also covers only selectors that return an unchanged pool member rather than repairing candidates or synthesizing a new artifact. Benchmarks therefore report candidate availability alongside selected-output performance under the relevant criterion, because the gap between the two can be large. Figure 3 recomputes the plateau described in section 3.2.2 from the released per-sample correctness labels of Brown et al. (2024). It plots coverage (the task-averaged pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) estimate) against the accuracy of majority voting over the same pools.

At 10,000 samples, the smaller model’s pools hold a correct answer for 98 percent of the problems, yet majority voting returns one for 43 percent, and 59 percent for the larger model. Beyond 256 samples the vote gains less than a point while coverage gains another 10. The repair agent’s coverage rises from 16 percent with one sample to 56 percent at 250, but that growth is usable only because tests can identify which candidate passes. Spending on breadth is therefore justified by the selector, not by the pool. Without a check that recognizes the correct candidate, the added samples raise the pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) ceiling without raising what the runtime returns.

↪ The distinction between pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) over a candidate pool and consistency over repeated system runs is developed in Potential and repeated-run consistency.

Each doubling squares the chance that all candidates fail, so the bars approach certainty.

Breadth has a price that pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) makes easy to compute. In best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling, the runtime generates \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidates for one decision and returns one that its selector admits. The pool is the same object pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) describes, so its availability is pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) with \(\IndependentAttemptCount = \CandidatePoolSize\), written pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) from here on. The notebook below prices that pool against the simplest alternative, generating one candidate at a time and stopping at the first pass, under the most favorable selection assumption, in which the verifier is the acceptance check itself.

Napkin Math 3.3: The price of a larger pool
Problem: Each candidate repair passes the task’s verifier with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\), independently for this task, and the verifier is the acceptance check. How many candidates per accepted repair does a parallel pool of \(\CandidatePoolSize\) spend, compared with sequential retry that stops at the first pass and gives up after \(\CandidatePoolSize\) attempts?

Variables: \(\AttemptPassProbability\) = 30 percent (hypothetical); pools of 1, 2, 4, and 8 candidates; a selector that returns a passing member whenever one exists.

Math: The formula pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize = 1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\CandidatePoolSize\) gives 30, 51, 76, and 94.2 percent for those pools. The last of 8 candidates adds \(\AttemptPassProbability(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^{\CandidatePoolSize-1}\), only 2.5 percentage points. The parallel pool always spends 8 candidates, or 8.5 per accepted repair. Sequential retry fails only when all 8 attempts fail, so it reaches the same 94.2 percent. Because each subsequent attempt runs only when all earlier attempts failed (with probabilities \(1, 1-\AttemptPassProbability, \dots, (1-\AttemptPassProbability)^{\CandidatePoolSize-1}\)), its expected spend is the finite geometric sum \([1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\CandidatePoolSize]/\AttemptPassProbability\) = 3.14 candidates. Dividing that expected spend by the pass probability \(1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\CandidatePoolSize\) cancels the numerator and leaves \(1/\AttemptPassProbability\) = 3.3 candidates per accepted repair.

Systems insight: At equal success, the parallel pool costs 2.5× as much per accepted repair; what it buys is one round instead of an expected 3.14 sequential generate-and-check rounds. Breadth buys latency, not cost. In \(\CostPerAcceptedTask = \mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\) it raises \(\AcceptanceProbability\) per round but raises the attempt cost faster. Size \(\CandidatePoolSize\) as the smallest pool whose pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) meets the target within the rounds the deadline allows, and stop growing it once the next candidate’s gain is worth less than its cost.

3.3.4 Balancing pool size against checking cost

Growing the candidate pool raises the chance that a valid repair enters the pool, but it also multiplies the checking load. Under a fixed deliberation budget, spending nearly everything on generation starves verification and forces the selector onto cheap, shallow heuristics. A smaller pool paired with a thorough check often beats a large pool paired with a weak selector; only when candidate generation itself is the bottleneck does a wider pool pay off right away. Comparing the two regimes therefore requires pricing generation and selection together rather than tuning either stage on its own.

Checker errors also compound as the pool grows, because each extra invalid candidate gives an imperfect verifier another chance to register a false pass. If a verifier has a 5 percent false acceptance rate (it passes 5 percent of invalid candidates, defined formally in section 3.4.2), testing twenty invalid candidates with independent check outcomes makes at least one false pass more likely than not (\(1 - (1 - 0.05)^{20} \approx 64\%\)). Whether the selector actually returns that false pass depends on what else sits in the pool and on the tie-breaking rule, a distinction the next section makes exact. The defect rate of the returned candidate depends on the generator’s base rate of valid candidates, the check’s false-acceptance and false-rejection rates, and how the selector chooses among passing candidates. When an adaptive search steers generation using verifier scores, it shifts all three quantities at once and can push candidates straight into the verifier’s blind spots.

Selection rules and pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) price the candidate pool under a stated check, but they treat the check itself as a black box. In practice, checks differ in whether they inspect finished artifacts or intermediate steps, how the generator’s base rate bounds the precision of a passing verdict, and how quickly search pressure exploits their blind spots. Designing a selector that deserves trust therefore requires looking inside the verification checks themselves.

3.4 Verifying Candidates

In a connection-pool repair, clean compilation confirms only syntax and types, and even a targeted concurrency test exercises only a handful of thread schedules and can miss a race condition. Every verification check answers a bounded question, never the whole question of whether a candidate is safe to ship. Engineers therefore have to decide which checks to run, whether to attach them to finished candidates or to intermediate steps, and how much trust to place in a passing verdict when valid candidates are rare.

3.4.1 Checking whole outputs vs. intermediate steps

Waiting until a candidate patch is completely written before running any check wastes every token spent elaborating a diagnosis that went wrong in step one, whereas scoring every intermediate step from scratch rescans the growing prefix again and again. An outcome check tests a completed candidate against an external rule, such as whether a patched service passes an end-to-end regression suite. An outcome reward model, by contrast, predicts a scalar quality score for the finished candidate, like the learned verifiers trained to rank candidate solutions in mathematical reasoning (Cobbe et al. 2021). The outcome check runs the artifact and reports what happened; the reward model only predicts how good the artifact looks.

2 Step-Level Labels (PRM800K): Outcome labels for code can be produced automatically by running tests. Step labels require a judgment on each intermediate step, and the PRM800K dataset used to train the process reward model of Lightman et al. (2024) contains about 800,000 human step-level labels. That labeling cost is one reason learned process scorers are scarcer than outcome checks.

Lightman, Hunter, Vineet Kosaraju, Yura Burda, Harri Edwards, Bowen Baker, Teddy Lee, Jan Leike, John Schulman, Ilya Sutskever, and Karl Cobbe. 2024. “Let’s Verify Step by Step.” The Twelfth International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Step-level checks evaluate intermediate claims or partial states before the candidate is complete. In the connection-pool repair, a step check can test whether a waiting worker receives a wake-up signal before the agent drafts a full timeout patch. If the trace refutes the timeout explanation, the runtime drops that branch immediately and saves the remaining generation tokens. Learned process scorers similarly score or prune partial reasoning traces, using low step scores as early warnings of downstream failure. Process supervision2 attaches feedback to discrete intermediate steps rather than waiting for the final answer (Uesato et al. 2022; Lightman et al. 2024). What separates outcome feedback from process feedback is where the signal attaches, not what the scorer reads, since a learned outcome scorer can also read the full reasoning trace before grading the final output.

Definition 3.3: Process reward model (PRM)

A process reward model (PRM) is a learned scorer that assigns a value to each intermediate step of a candidate trajectory, conditioned on the task context and the preceding steps.

  1. Significance: Step scores allow search algorithms to prune unpromising branches early, keeping beam search and tree search, the search procedures that extend only the best-scoring partial candidates (section 3.6.1), within finite computational budgets.
  2. Distinction: An outcome reward model evaluates only terminal states; a process reward model assigns credit across intermediate steps. This early steering capability requires dense step-level supervision, which costs substantially more to gather than automated test outcomes.
  3. Common pitfall: Mistaking a high step score for verified correctness. A process reward model provides heuristic guidance for ranking and pruning, but it offers no formal guarantees and satisfies no closure evidence level.

Each granularity catches failures the other misses. An outcome check tests the assembled artifact end to end, catching integration bugs that look harmless inside any single step. A process check catches errors early and stops broken branches from burning more generation tokens, but checking too often adds scorer overhead and risks pruning good branches when step scores are noisy. Choosing how often to check balances saved generation tokens against scorer cost and false rejections.

Step-scoring cost also depends on whether the serving engine reuses prefix state across steps. Suppose a scorer reads a task prompt of \(\SequenceLength\) tokens plus the candidate’s growing prefix after each of its \(\ScoredStepCount\) steps, where each step adds \(\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\) tokens. Scoring each cumulative prefix from scratch, without reusing key-value cache state from earlier steps, processes

\[ \sum_{j=1}^{\ScoredStepCount}\left(\SequenceLength + \ScoredStepIndex\,\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\right) = \ScoredStepCount \SequenceLength + \frac{\ScoredStepCount(\ScoredStepCount+1)}{2}\,\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength \tag{2}\]

scorer input tokens, which grows quadratically with the number of steps \(\ScoredStepCount\). The beam-search calculation later in this chapter (notebook 3.6) charges the \(\ScoredStepIndex\,\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\) term of equation 2, with the task prompt \(\SequenceLength\) excluded, for every extension scored at step \(\ScoredStepIndex\). When the serving engine caches prefixes across steps, incremental scoring reads only the new \(\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\) tokens at each step and removes the quadratic growth; scorers that must re-read the whole candidate without prefix caching pay the full sum.

Table 4 compares four common kinds of check by what they inspect, where they can be fooled, whether they are deterministic, and which closure evidence level (The invariant closure principle) they reach. Compilers and sealed test suites establish bounded, reproducible facts within their scope, whereas learned scorers only rank or prune candidates.

3.4.2 How base rates limit check precision

When only 5 percent of generated patches actually fix a bug, even a test suite that falsely passes just 2 percent of broken patches leaves nearly a third of all passing candidates defective. The reliability question for any check is what share of the candidates that pass it are genuinely valid. Call meeting the full task specification \(\text{Valid}\) and receiving a positive check verdict \(\text{PASS}\). Let \(\ValidCandidateRate = \Pr(\text{Valid})\) be the base rate of valid candidates in the generator’s output distribution, \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = \Pr(\text{PASS}\mid\neg\text{Valid})\) the false acceptance rate, and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = \Pr(\neg\text{PASS}\mid\text{Valid})\) the false rejection rate. Here “acceptance” means passing the internal verifier, not authorization to ship to production. The valid-candidate rate \(\ValidCandidateRate\) is not the test-pass probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) used in pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\): \(\ValidCandidateRate\) is the chance of meeting the full task specification, whereas \(\AttemptPassProbability\) is the chance of passing the stated tests. A perfect check makes the two coincide; an imperfect check separates them, and the precision formula below measures the gap.

The total probability that a random candidate passes the verifier is \(\VerifierPassProbability=\ValidCandidateRate(1-\VerifierFalseRejectionRate)+(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\,\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\). Applying the same Bayes’ rule that gave the defect share among one checker’s passing patches (equation 2.4) gives the precision of positive verdicts:

\[ \VerifierPrecision \equiv \Pr(\text{Valid}\mid\text{PASS}) =\frac{\ValidCandidateRate(1-\VerifierFalseRejectionRate)}{\ValidCandidateRate(1-\VerifierFalseRejectionRate)+(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\,\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate}. \tag{3}\]

At a low valid rate, even a strict check passes many invalid candidates.

When valid candidates are rare, even a check with a small false acceptance rate produces a passing pool where broken candidates make up a large share, the same base-rate trap that Self-review and external evidence showed for a single checked patch. Applied across a candidate pool, this is the verification asymmetry (principle 3) in quantitative form. Generating a passing candidate and knowing that a passing candidate is valid are different events, and the base rate and checker error rates, not the pool size, bound how far a pass can be trusted. Both error rates therefore have to be measured on real tasks for each model family and test harness.

While equation 3 describes the pool of passing candidates as a whole, the reliability of the single candidate the runtime returns depends on the selection rule. Suppose candidate-check pairs are drawn independently and identically for a fixed task under constant error rates. Suppose also that the runtime checks the pool in a fixed order unrelated to quality, returns the first candidate that passes, and abstains if none passes. Under that first-passing rule, the probability that a returned candidate is valid equals \(\VerifierPrecision\): learning that earlier candidates in the list failed does not change the conditional validity of the first one that passes. Picking the candidate with the highest continuous reward score behaves differently, because maximizing a noisy score actively concentrates positive scoring errors, as the next subsection shows.

Napkin Math 3.4: Yield and risk under asymmetric verifier error
Problem: A repair policy generates all 64 candidates before selection. It uses the first-passing rule above and abstains when no candidate passes. What does additional breadth buy, and what risk remains?

Variables: All rates are hypothetical. Each candidate contains 1,024 tokens, billed at $15 per million output tokens. The valid-candidate rate is 5 percent, false rejection is 10 percent, and false acceptance is 2 percent. Suppose a separately authorized deployment policy deploys the selected candidate without further checking. A defective deployment incurs $500 in incident loss. For this calculation alone, valid deployments and abstentions incur zero incident loss, and missed repairs and delay are left unpriced.

Math: The expected passing counts are 2.88 valid candidates and 1.216 invalid candidates, totaling 4.096. Equation 3 yields 70.31 percent precision. Conditional on making a selection, the probability of a defective selection is therefore 29.69 percent, with expected incident loss $148.44 per selected deployment.

The probability of any selection is \(1-(1-\VerifierPassProbability)^\CandidatePoolSize\), or 98.55 percent; the policy abstains on 1.45 percent of batches. Multiplying selection coverage by conditional defect probability gives 29.26 percent defective selections per task attempt and $146.28 expected incident loss per attempt, including abstentions. Generating the entire batch costs $0.98 in output charges. Input charges, checking, and other operational costs are omitted. A sequential policy that stops generation at the first pass would have a different generation bill.

Systems insight: Expected incident loss per attempt is about 149 times the output bill, so the acceptance policy, not the token price, sets the cost of this task. Precision responds to two knobs of similar strength: halving the false acceptance rate raises it from 70.31 percent to 82.6 percent, and doubling the valid-candidate rate raises it to 83.3 percent. More candidates move neither. At a low base rate, spend on whichever of a better generator or a stricter check is cheaper, and price false rejections too when a missed valid repair is costly.

With \(\ValidCandidateRate\), \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\), and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\) held fixed, growing the pool size \(\CandidatePoolSize\) does not change precision \(\VerifierPrecision\). A wider pool raises the chance of finding at least one passing candidate, but it also raises the chance that the batch holds at least one false pass: \(1-[1-(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\,\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate]^\CandidatePoolSize\). In the calculation above, that false-pass exposure reaches 70.70 percent, while the probability of actually returning a defective candidate is 29.26 percent. The gap arises because under a first-passing rule a false pass later in the batch does no harm if an earlier valid candidate passes first. Pool availability, passing-set precision, and deployed defect risk are three distinct numbers.

3.4.3 How search exploits imperfect checks

The more candidates a search generates and ranks against an imperfect scorer or test suite, the more likely the winning candidate is one that exploited a scorer blind spot or deleted a failing assertion rather than fixing the bug. Fixed error rates describe passive sampling from a fixed distribution, but adaptive search changes the candidates themselves. When a search policy steers generation or picks the maximum of a noisy score, it pushes candidates into regions where the evaluator is poorly calibrated, so the baseline rates \(\ValidCandidateRate\), \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\), and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\) no longer hold for the candidates that reach the end of the search. Optimizing against an imperfect proxy breaks down in three distinct ways (Manheim and Garrabrant 2018). Manheim and Garrabrant classify them as forms of Goodhart’s law.3 In regressional Goodhart, picking the highest score selects for positive measurement noise. In extremal Goodhart, correlations that hold on typical candidates break down in the tail reached by search. In adversarial Goodhart, candidate code tampers with or bypasses the test harness.

Manheim, David, and Scott Garrabrant. 2018. “Categorizing Goodhart’s Law.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1803.04585.

3 Goodhart’s Law: The economist Charles Goodhart stated it in 1975 about monetary policy: any observed statistical regularity tends to collapse once pressure is placed upon it for control purposes (Goodhart 1984). The anthropologist Marilyn Strathern later gave it the form usually quoted today, that when a measure becomes a target it ceases to be a good measure (Strathern 1997). A test suite used to select patches is such a regularity, a reliable description of past candidates that weakens once search optimizes against it.

Goodhart, Charles A. E. 1984. “Problems of Monetary Management: The UK Experience.” In Monetary Theory and Practice. Palgrave. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-349-17295-5_4.
Strathern, Marilyn. 1997. “‘Improving Ratings’: Audit in the British University System.” European Review 5 (3): 305–21. https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1234-981X(199707)5:3<305::AID-EURO184>3.0.CO;2-4.

The regressional effect can be written out directly. Suppose a learned scorer reports an estimated utility \(\EstimatedCandidateUtility_i = \CandidateUtility_i + \CandidateScoringError_i\) for candidate \(\CandidateIndex\), where \(\CandidateUtility_i\) is true task utility and \(\CandidateScoringError_i\) is an independent scoring error with mean zero and standard deviation \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\) (scoring noise, distinct from the per-turn error rate in horizon compounding). Picking \(\arg\max_i \EstimatedCandidateUtility_i\) maximizes the sum of utility and noise, so the winning candidate is disproportionately one that drew a large positive error. For independent Gaussian errors, the expected maximum error across \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidates scales for large \(\CandidatePoolSize\) as

\[ \mathbb{E}\Bigl[\max_{i \le \CandidatePoolSize} \CandidateScoringError_i\Bigr] \approx \ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\sqrt{2\ln \CandidatePoolSize}. \tag{4}\]

In units of \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\), the exact expectation is 1.0 at 4 candidates and 2.3 at 64 candidates, where the asymptotic formula gives 2.9.4 That expected maximum is an upper bound on the winner’s expected scoring error, reached when all candidates have the same true utility. When true utilities vary, selection responds to both utility and noise (Exercise 8 derives the winner’s error for a Gaussian utility model). An acceptance threshold calibrated on single, unselected completions therefore cannot be reused unmodified on argmax-selected winners.

4 Extreme-Value Asymptotics: The form \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\sqrt{2\ln \CandidatePoolSize}\) is only the leading term of the Gaussian maximum, and convergence is slow, which is why the chapter computes the exact expectation beside it. The Gaussian is also a favorable case. Scoring errors with heavier tails, such as a learned judge that occasionally misjudges badly, push the selected error up faster than \(\sqrt{\ln \CandidatePoolSize}\), so the drift in a real pool can exceed this estimate.

A rising scoring error on the winner does not by itself mean that true utility is falling. In Exercise 8(b), independent Gaussian utility and noise make both the winner’s expected utility and its expected scoring error rise with pool size. Empirical studies of best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling under learned reward models show a sharper divergence: the proxy score keeps climbing while a held-out reference score rises, flattens, and then drops (Gao et al. 2023). That downturn comes from tail miscalibration or exploitable gaps in the proxy, not from equation 4 alone. Figure 4 sketches the pattern, with the winner’s proxy score rising (dashed blue curve) while the held-out reference score peaks and turns down (solid green curve).

Checker blind spots compound across large pools by the same binomial arithmetic as pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\). If an unmodeled edge case or loophole appears in a fraction \(\ExploitCandidateFraction\) of single draws, a pool of \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidates contains at least one loophole-exploiting candidate with probability \(1-(1-\ExploitCandidateFraction)^\CandidatePoolSize\). A loophole that shows up in only 0.5 percent of individual samples, rare enough to miss during single-call testing, appears in a batch of 64 candidates with probability 27.4 percent. Once a broken candidate slips through that loophole and receives a passing mark, competitive selection promotes it. Checker accuracy measured on unselected outputs therefore overstates accuracy under search, and the gap widens as the pool grows.

In the connection-pool repair, a patch can pass the test suite either by fixing the missing wake-up notification or by deleting the unit-test assertion that checks for blocked worker threads. Both edits raise the test score, and only the first fixes the bug. The candidate under test must therefore be isolated from the verifier so that generated code cannot edit test files or forge pass signals.

↪ Grader isolation enforces this separation down to the candidate’s process and interpreter, as developed in Grader isolation.

Isolating the grader stops tampering, but it does not widen what the test suite covers: a sealed regression suite still misses untested thread schedules, and a learned judge can still prefer confident, broken code. Evaluation must therefore test the outputs that the complete search policy produces at its deployed budget, counting false acceptances and abstentions explicitly. Adding confidence requires an independent check that covers a different failure mode rather than a second correlated scorer.

Verification also produces concrete intermediate facts that later turns can reuse, including refuted diagnoses, verified invariants, and passing test subsets. When a check refutes one assumption inside a multi-part candidate plan, the runtime does not need to throw away components that never depended on that assumption, provided it records which parts of the plan rest on which premises.

3.5 Revising Candidate Plans

When a targeted test refutes the initial diagnosis inside a multi-part repair plan, throwing away the entire plan wastes valid work such as API compatibility checks, whereas keeping the plan unchanged leaves the patch and test selection resting on a disproven premise. Suppose the repair agent forms a diagnosis, drafts a patch, checks public API constraints, and picks a test plan for a connection-pool leak, and a targeted test then refutes the diagnosis. To avoid both waste and stale conclusions, the runtime needs to identify which parts of the candidate depend on the refuted premise before deciding what the next model call should regenerate.

A revisable candidate plan records those dependencies explicitly. Instead of logging past actions, it tracks unexecuted candidate artifacts together with the evidence or upstream artifacts each one relies on. When new evidence refutes a premise, the runtime invalidates and regenerates only the affected subgraph while keeping supported artifacts intact, and the replacement artifacts then go through their own checks. This design comes directly from truth maintenance systems, which record explicit justifications for each conclusion so that retracting a premise automatically invalidates every step built on it (Doyle 1979).

3.5.1 Tracking dependencies inside a candidate

Suppose the first repair candidate blames the connection-pool failure on an overly short client timeout. To know which parts of that candidate survive when a test refutes the timeout hypothesis, the runtime represents the candidate as a directed acyclic graph with a directed edge from each prerequisite to the artifact that depends on it (table 5). Here the source excerpt, initial failure log, API declaration, and test inventory are supplied inputs, whereas the diagnosis, patch candidate, check selection, and API compatibility notes are generated artifacts. The graph records which components depend on which, whether or not the model generated them in that order.

Now suppose the waiter-and-return test shows that an idle connection entered the pool while a waiting worker stayed blocked past the notification point. That observation refutes the timeout diagnosis and points to a missed wake-up notification instead. Because both the timeout patch and its check selection depend on the refuted diagnosis, the runtime marks them invalid and schedules them for replacement. The source excerpt stays valid because the repository file has not changed, and the API compatibility notes stay valid because the public interface constraints have not changed. The runtime builds the next prompt from the still-valid inputs plus the new test observation, asks for a new diagnosis, patch, and check selection, and runs checks on the new artifacts.

That selective reuse is only as sound as the recorded edges. A graph with a missing edge keeps stale conclusions just as an unmanaged prompt buffer does: if the compatibility notes silently depended on helper signatures that the new patch changes, leaving out that edge keeps broken compatibility claims in place. Dependency tracking pays off when maintaining the graph and walking its edges costs less in time and tokens than regenerating the unaffected branches from scratch.

3.5.2 Invalidating stale and refuted evidence

Walking the dependency graph requires distinguishing an actively refuted claim from an observation whose preconditions no longer match the current candidate. A failing test directly refutes the claim that a patch fixes the bug, whereas a passing test from an earlier candidate remains a true record of what that earlier patch did while saying nothing about the edited patch now on the table. Reusing the earlier pass after the code changes would let an unverified edit slip through on stale evidence, whereas treating an expired observation as a direct contradiction would falsely rule out a working hypothesis.

To keep reuse sound, every recorded observation must carry its full provenance: the exact source revision or patch digest, test suite version, compiler flags, and runtime environment. Likewise, an observation from reading a file must bind to the cryptographic hash of the file contents when read, not to a file path whose contents a later step might overwrite.

When an input changes or a premise fails, the runtime marks that node unsupported and walks the directed edges to find every downstream artifact that needs review. If node \(\InvalidatedArtifactNode\) loses support, the invalidated set is its reachable descendants in the graph: \(\text{Desc}(\InvalidatedArtifactNode) = \{\ReachableArtifactNode \mid \InvalidatedArtifactNode \rightsquigarrow \ReachableArtifactNode\}\), where \(\InvalidatedArtifactNode \rightsquigarrow \ReachableArtifactNode\) means a directed path runs from \(\InvalidatedArtifactNode\) to \(\ReachableArtifactNode\). Breadth-first search finds \(\text{Desc}(\InvalidatedArtifactNode)\) in time linear in the vertices and edges.5 Every node outside \(\text{Desc}(\InvalidatedArtifactNode)\) keeps its support, including separate subgraphs such as the API compatibility notes. Walking a candidate graph of dozens or hundreds of nodes, like the one in table 5, takes a negligible fraction of the illustrative 21.07 ms needed to decode a single token. The real engineering work is therefore recording the dependency edges accurately rather than traversing them. In the connection-pool repair, the waiter-and-return test contradicts the timeout diagnosis, and graph traversal marks its descendants (the patch and check selection) for review. The next call regenerates only the diagnosis and that branch from the retained inputs (figure 5).

5 Dependency-Driven Rebuilding: Build tools solved the same problem for files. Make rebuilds a target only when one of its declared prerequisites is newer than the target (Feldman 1979), and its characteristic failure is the one described here, in which an undeclared dependency leaves a stale target that looks up to date. The saving is only as sound as the recorded edges.

Feldman, Stuart I. 1979. “Make — a Program for Maintaining Computer Programs.” Software: Practice and Experience 9 (4): 255–65. https://doi.org/10.1002/spe.4380090402.

Content hashes and environment digests make invalidation deterministic only when the recorded boundary captures every input that matters. An integration test that calls a live network service can change its outcome even when local files stay bit-identical, whereas editing an unrelated README file should not invalidate unit-test results whose code and inputs did not change. Sound reuse therefore depends on checking the full execution context (sandbox isolation, mocks, and declared inputs) rather than file timestamps or paths alone.

Candidate plans must also be kept separate from live environment state. The dependency graph tracks uncommitted candidate artifacts and their justifications; reading files, writing to disk, or running binaries in a sandbox changes real system state. Dropping an invalidated node from the plan graph does not roll back a modified file or free a running container, so the runtime must handle rollback and cleanup through separate recovery routines.

↪ Compensation and cleanup of external effects that outlive an abandoned candidate branch are developed in Branch-scoped compensation.

3.5.3 Choosing how much of a plan to regenerate

Once graph traversal marks the invalidated nodes, the runtime must decide whether to replace a single node, rebuild its entire downstream subgraph, or stop deliberating altogether. Replacing a single step in isolation fails when the new artifact changes an interface that downstream steps rely on, whereas regenerating the whole plan from scratch wastes tokens and risks breaking parts that already worked.
