To scope a revision cleanly, the runtime builds a prompt with the task constraints, the still-valid upstream frontier, the observation that refuted the old step, and the exact nodes marked for replacement. In the connection-pool repair, the prompt asks for a new diagnosis, patch, and check selection while keeping the unchanged source excerpt and API notes. When the model returns replacement artifacts, the runtime checks both that they fit the retained artifacts and that their own prerequisites are met, because a locally valid replacement can still clash with preserved downstream nodes.

Depending on how much support the new observation removes, the runtime chooses among three responses. Figure 6 orders these three responses around two questions, whether the dependents of the affected artifact still have their needs met and whether the task’s own premises still hold:

  1. A local repair replaces a single node while keeping its interface and contracts unchanged so that its dependents stay valid, such as fixing a malformed shell flag in a test command without changing what the test checks.
  2. A subgraph resynthesis replaces a node and all of its descendants, feeding the model the intact upstream frontier and asking it to rebuild the path to the goal. In the connection-pool repair, this replaces the diagnosis, patch, and test selection while keeping the source excerpt and API compatibility notes.
  3. Stopping deliberation applies when new evidence refutes a premise of the task itself, such as a missing host library or credentials without write access. No plan revision can fix a broken task premise, so the runtime stops deliberating and escalates or reports the task unresolved instead of spending more tokens.

Smallest is not always best. When new evidence overturns an early framing assumption or the dependency graph has grown tangled, regenerating the whole candidate from scratch often produces a cleaner, more consistent result than patching a large subgraph, at the cost of re-emitting settled material and risking regressions in parts that already worked. Choosing between scoped revision and full regeneration weighs the share of the graph that was invalidated against the tokens, time, and checks needed to rebuild and verify the candidate.

Napkin Math 3.5: Output cost of a local plan revision
Problem: Suppose a candidate contains 12 nodes, each requiring 150 generated tokens. New evidence leaves 3 nodes needing replacement. How much output does scoped regeneration avoid if either approach produces a candidate adequate for the same subsequent checks?

Math: Multiplying the complete node count by the output per node gives 1,800 tokens for full regeneration. Multiplying the replacement count by the same output allowance gives 450 tokens for scoped regeneration. The scoped request generates 25 percent of the full request’s output, avoiding 75 percent of that output under the equal-node-size assumption.

Systems insight: Scoped regeneration saves the exact fraction of the plan that keeps its support. That saving shrinks linearly as the refuted premise reaches more descendants and disappears when every node depends on it, so once most of the plan rests on the rejected premise, full regeneration costs little more and is simpler to check. This count covers generated plan tokens only; both options still pay for prompt input, integration, and verification checks.

These output savings have to be weighed against the work of maintaining dependencies, building scoped prompts, and checking hybrid plans. Once a candidate plan passes verification and crosses into execution, it stops being an uncommitted candidate graph and becomes live workflow state that the harness executes and durable execution checkpoints across machine failures.

↪ Persisting committed plan state across worker and infrastructure faults is developed in Durable Execution.

3.5.4 Preventing revision thrashing

Even when each scoped revision saves tokens compared with full regeneration, a revision loop without stopping rules can still oscillate between two diagnoses, produce cosmetically different patches with the same bug, or regenerate plans in response to harmless compiler warnings. The runtime therefore checks whether a proposed revision responds to new evidence that actually refutes a premise, rather than treating every warning or score fluctuation as a reason to replan.

To prevent thrashing, the runtime enforces hard ceilings on model calls, total tokens, check runs, and wall-clock time. Every revision attempt, including a failed local repair, draws from that allowance, and the same ceilings bound the search as a whole (section 3.6.6). When the budget runs out, the loop stops and reports the task unresolved or escalates to a human operator without merging the unverified patch. Seeing the same or structurally equivalent candidate under unchanged observations also triggers an immediate stop; revisiting an earlier idea is justified only when a new test observation has changed the evidence.

Dependency graphs let the runtime revise a single candidate without throwing away its valid parts, but an agent often holds several partial candidates at once. Deciding which partial candidate to extend next, how to keep concurrent branches from corrupting one another’s workspaces, and when to stop searching altogether turns revision into bounded search.

3.6 Bounded Search

The repair agent now holds several partial candidates at once, some freshly revised and some untouched. At each step it can sample a fresh patch from the root, extend an existing partial candidate by one segment, or run a diagnostic probe to separate competing explanations. Every choice draws from the same task budget while creating different scheduling dependencies. A probe that gates the next code extension sits on the critical path. Independent candidate extensions can run in parallel across worker slots. Dropping a dead-end branch still forfeits the tokens and sandbox time already spent on it. A search policy therefore needs a pruning structure, workspace isolation between branches, separate accounts for tokens, time, and memory, reservations for in-flight work, and an ordered stopping rule.

3.6.1 Where each search structure prunes

Decoding a candidate patch to full length after its first line already rests on a broken premise wastes every remaining token on that branch, yet checking after every short segment stalls generation behind a scorer. Search structures trade those two costs by choosing when to check and discard partial candidates. Flat sampling waits until the end: in best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\), the runtime dispatches up to \(\CandidatePoolSize\) independent generation calls and runs its selector only after all of them finish. Because the calls do not depend on one another, free serving capacity turns directly into lower latency through batching, provided the cluster can run all \(\CandidatePoolSize\) streams without slowing per-token decoding, at the price of decoding every flawed branch to completion.

Beam search6 moves that check inside the generation loop. The runtime keeps a frontier of at most \(\BeamWidth\) partial candidates (the beam width). At each step it generates a fixed number of extensions per retained candidate, scores the extended prefixes, and keeps only the top \(\BeamWidth\) survivors for the next step. If fewer than \(\BeamWidth\) survive a check, only those survivors advance, and the root starts as a single prompt. Step scores can combine model log-probabilities, a learned process scorer, or a deterministic check. While \(\BeamWidth\) caps the survivors carried between stages, peak memory during expansion must hold \(\BeamWidth\) times the branching factor in active extensions along with their KV caches, scores, and parent pointers.

6 Beam Search: The method first appeared in the HARPY speech recognition system, which advanced only a small set of the best-scoring partial paths at each step instead of the full search space (Lowerre 1976). It accepted a bounded risk of pruning the eventual best path in exchange for bounded time and memory, the same trade a runtime makes when it retains a fixed number of partial candidates.

Lowerre, Bruce T. 1976. “The HARPY Speech Recognition System.” PhD thesis, Carnegie Mellon University; Carnegie Mellon University.

Pruning at each step trades generation tokens for scoring work and bets on the accuracy of the step scorer. If a noisy step score drops a prefix that would have led to a working patch, a fixed-width beam search never visits that branch again unless backtracking is added. Conversely, killing a broken prefix early saves all the tokens and final test runs its completion would have cost. Step pruning pays off when scoring costs less than the avoided downstream generation and the step check rarely rejects valid prefixes.

Rather than expanding every surviving candidate equally at every depth, tree search over intermediate reasoning steps (Yao et al. 2023) directs the next extension to whichever partial candidate looks most promising or most uncertain. Monte Carlo tree search (Coulom 2006; Kocsis and Szepesv’ari 2006) tracks visit counts and average leaf scores along the edges of the search tree. Its PUCT selection rule7 from AlphaGo (Silver et al. 2016) walks down from the root by picking at each partial state \(\PolicyObservedState\) the extension \(\PolicyAction\) that maximizes

7 From UCT to PUCT: The UCT rule treated each node’s choice of extension as a multi-armed bandit, adding an exploration bonus that shrinks as an extension accumulates visits (Kocsis and Szepesv’ari 2006). PUCT weights that bonus by a predictor’s prior over the arms (Rosin 2011), which is what lets a language model’s own step probabilities concentrate a small budget on plausible extensions. A miscalibrated prior steers visits away from correct but unlikely steps just as effectively.

Kocsis, Levente, and Csaba Szepesv’ari. 2006. “Bandit Based Monte-Carlo Planning.” European Conference on Machine Learning (ECML), 282–93.
Rosin, Christopher D. 2011. “Multi-Armed Bandits with Episode Context.” Annals of Mathematics and Artificial Intelligence 61 (3): 203–30. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10472-011-9258-6.

\[ \TreeSearchMeanReturn(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction) + \TreeSearchExplorationWeight\,\TreeSearchPriorDistribution(\PolicyAction \mid \PolicyObservedState)\,\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{\SiblingPolicyAction} \TreeSearchVisitCount(\PolicyObservedState,\SiblingPolicyAction)}}{1 + \TreeSearchVisitCount(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction)}, \tag{5}\]

where \(\TreeSearchMeanReturn(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction)\) is the mean leaf score seen on visits through extension \(\PolicyAction\), and \(\TreeSearchVisitCount(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction)\) is its visit count. The term \(\TreeSearchPriorDistribution(\PolicyAction \mid \PolicyObservedState)\) is a prior distribution over extensions (such as the generator’s probability for that step), and \(\TreeSearchExplorationWeight\) is a positive weight that balances high-scoring branches against plausible, less-visited ones. The exploration ratio grows with the square root of total visits across all sibling extensions at \(\PolicyObservedState\) while shrinking linearly with \(1 + \TreeSearchVisitCount(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction)\), so repeatedly visiting one branch diminishes its own bonus while raising the bonus of plausible siblings that have not yet been tried. The leaf value \(\TreeSearchLeafScore\) is the score assigned to a newly expanded partial candidate by a process scorer or step check, and \(\TreeSearchMeanReturn\) averages those leaf values across visits. When a new leaf returns value \(\TreeSearchLeafScore\), every edge along the path back to the root increments its visit count (\(\TreeSearchVisitCount \leftarrow \TreeSearchVisitCount + 1\)) and updates its running mean (\(\TreeSearchMeanReturn \leftarrow \TreeSearchMeanReturn + (\TreeSearchLeafScore - \TreeSearchMeanReturn)/\TreeSearchVisitCount\)). If \(\TreeSearchLeafScore\) comes from a noisy learned scorer, \(\TreeSearchMeanReturn\) inherits the selection optimism of equation 4 and pulls future visits toward branches whose early scores were inflated by noise. Tree search therefore helps only when its smarter allocation saves more work than leaf scoring and tree bookkeeping add.

The three structures differ in where they prune (figure 7). Best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) waits until all candidates finish, beam search prunes at every step boundary, and tree search expands one favored leaf at a time while updating visit counts and mean values along its path. Those pruning points set each structure’s concurrency, live state, and prefix sharing (table 6).

↪ Radix prefix caching, which lets later requests for a branch reuse the KV state of its shared ancestors, is developed in Radix prefix lookup.

3.6.2 How step pruning bounds tree growth

A search tree that expands every node into 4 branches across 10 steps without pruning produces 1,048,576 leaves and quickly exhausts memory and token budgets. Step pruning bounds that growth. Suppose each retained node proposes \(\SearchBranchingFactor\) child extensions and a step check independently rejects each child with probability \(\ChildPruningProbability\). The expected number of surviving children per expanded node, the effective branching factor, is

\[ \EffectiveBranchingFactor = \SearchBranchingFactor\,(1 - \ChildPruningProbability). \tag{6}\]

Under equation 6, the expected frontier width at depth \(\SearchDepthIndex\) is \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor^{\,\SearchDepthIndex}\). Because each surviving node at depth \(\SearchDepthIndex-1\) generates and checks \(\SearchBranchingFactor\) children, the expected total number of generated nodes through depth \(\SearchDepth\) is \(\sum_{\SearchDepthIndex=1}^{\SearchDepth} \SearchBranchingFactor\,\EffectiveBranchingFactor^{\,\SearchDepthIndex-1}\). This is a Galton–Watson branching process.8 When \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor < 1\), which requires a rejection rate above \(1 - 1/\SearchBranchingFactor\), the frontier shrinks geometrically with depth and dies out on its own. When \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor > 1\), the expected frontier still grows exponentially, so hard ceilings on depth, tokens, or time must stop the search.

8 Galton–Watson Process: The model comes from a Victorian question about the extinction of family surnames, analyzed in 1875 (Watson and Galton 1875). That analysis wrongly concluded that every line dies out; the correct result is the threshold used here, under which extinction is certain when the mean number of surviving offspring is at most one (excluding the degenerate case of exactly one child every time) and avoidable when it exceeds one. For a search tree the surviving offspring are the children that pass the step check, so the rejection rate decides which side of the threshold the search lands on.

Watson, Henry William, and Francis Galton. 1875. “On the Probability of the Extinction of Families.” The Journal of the Anthropological Institute of Great Britain and Ireland 4. https://doi.org/10.2307/2841222.

Suppose each node proposes 5 extensions, the step check rejects 68 percent of them, and the search runs to depth 3. The effective branching factor is 1.6. Because the 68 percent rejection rate sits below the critical rate of 80 percent for \(\SearchBranchingFactor =\) 5, the tree remains supercritical (\(\EffectiveBranchingFactor > 1\)). Even so, an unpruned tree across three steps generates 155 nodes and ends with 125 leaves. The pruned tree generates an expected 25.8 nodes and ends with 4.1 leaves, cutting node generations by 83.4 percent. The total saving beats the 68 percent single-step rejection rate because rejecting a parent also removes the entire subtree beneath it. With equal-sized steps, generated tokens drop by the same fraction, though each rejected node still pays for its own generation and step check.

Each rejected child also removes the subtree it would have grown.

Two practical limits qualify this calculation. First, a step check that falsely rejects valid steps kills good branches permanently, so aggressive pruning helps only when false rejections are rare. Second, when a step needs just one surviving child to move forward, proposing children one at a time takes \(1/(1 - \ChildPruningProbability)\) tries in expectation, whereas proposing a batch of \(\ChildBatchSize\) children in parallel takes \(1/(1 - \ChildPruningProbability^{\,\ChildBatchSize})\) rounds of \(\ChildBatchSize\) children each. Batching cuts wall-clock rounds at the price of generating more total children, the same latency-versus-work trade that notebook 3.3 priced for best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling.

3.6.3 Isolating workspaces across search branches

If one search branch edits files in a shared workspace and a sibling branch then runs its tests against that modified working tree, the sibling’s test results no longer reflect its own proposed patch. Although a partial candidate is just data in memory while being decoded, testing it requires running code against a real filesystem and process state. Every branch that writes files or mutates state therefore needs its own isolated container, copy-on-write filesystem snapshot, or deterministic rollback before another branch runs, so that each observation belongs strictly to the candidate and workspace snapshot that produced it.

↪ Copy-on-write task workspaces that keep each run’s file modifications private are developed in Copy-on-write provisioning.

Isolating branches costs real system resources even when most branches are later pruned. Concurrent sandboxes consume host RAM, disk volumes, process slots, and network bandwidth, while their test logs accumulate in the runtime’s state. Even read-only diagnostic commands need sandboxed permissions so they cannot leak host credentials or environment variables into the search trace. Pruning a branch drops it from the search frontier, but backtracking in the search tree cannot unsend an outbound network call or undo a write to an external database. Sandbox boundaries block those external writes during search, and any external action the runtime does permit has to be budgeted as irreversible.

3.6.4 Accounting for the whole search

Collapsing tokens, test runtimes, and memory into a single composite “search cost” hides whichever physical limit the search will hit first, because a beam search that cuts generated output tokens nearly in half can still cost more in dollars once repeated prompt reads are counted. A search runtime therefore keeps four separate accounts: money spent, physical work by unit, elapsed wall-clock time, and peak live state. Money is the one common unit that adds up across generator tokens, scorer calls, and tool runs. Physical work, by contrast, comes in non-interchangeable kinds: prompt tokens, output tokens, scorer tokens, test executions, and sandbox startups each load different hardware resources, and adding model FLOPs to test seconds produces a meaningless number.

Wall-clock time depends on how the scheduler maps those operations onto hardware: sequential stages add along the critical path, concurrent branches overlap up to the available worker slots, and requests beyond those slots wait in waves. Peak live state is a fourth, separate limit, set by what must sit in memory and storage at the same instant (active KV caches, candidate trees, test logs, and live sandboxes) rather than the cumulative work over the whole run. Saving output tokens by pruning early often adds prompt re-reads, scoring barriers, or concurrent state, so keeping the four accounts separate shows which resource actually constrains the policy.

Napkin Math 3.6: Flat sampling vs. bounded beam expansion
Problem: A coding agent generates candidate patches in 4 segments of 128 tokens. Compare 16 complete candidates with beam width 4 and 3 extensions per retained prefix. How do early pruning and repeated scoring change the resource totals?

Setup: Every generator request supplies the same 4,096-token task prompt plus its retained generated prefix. The illustrative per-token generation time from Estimating per-call hardware work is 21.075 ms. Assume 8 generation streams can maintain that rate concurrently. The scorer reads the entire generated prefix, excluding the common task prompt, and processes requests serially at a fixed 15 ms per request regardless of prefix length. The growth with prefix length that equation 2 describes therefore appears in the scorer’s token bill rather than in this stipulated time. Each final test takes 0.5 s, with 8 isolated runs available concurrently. Generation, scoring, and final testing do not overlap in this schedule.

Hypothetical prices per million tokens are $3 for generator input, $15 for output, and $0.50 for scorer input. Input charges include every supplied token, without a prefix-reuse discount. The timing subtotal omits prefill, queueing, request transfer, workspace preparation, and cleanup. It is not a prediction of complete service latency, and the token-charge subtotal excludes execution and infrastructure charges.

Math: Multiplying 16 candidates by 512 output tokens per candidate gives 8,192 output tokens. Multiplying the same candidate count by the common prompt length gives 65,536 input tokens. Generation occupies 2 waves of complete candidates, followed by 2 test waves. Multiplying the generation waves by candidate length and the per-token allowance, then adding the test waves times test duration, gives 22.58 s. Multiplying each token count by its corresponding price gives a token-charge subtotal of $0.3195.

The beam expands frontiers of 3, 9, 12, 12 extensions in successive stages, because the root expands 3 ways in stage 1, those 3 prefixes expand to 9 extensions in stage 2, and pruning retains at most the beam width (4 prefixes) from stage 2 onward so each later stage expands 4 \(\times\) 3 = 12 extensions. Adding these counts gives 36 newly generated segments, and multiplying by the segment length gives 4,608 output tokens. For each request, generator input is the common prompt plus the preceding generated segments, and summing across requests gives 156,288 input tokens. The scorer instead reads each complete generated prefix, including its new segment. Summing each stage’s extension count times its prefix length gives 13,440 scorer-input tokens. Because stages whose frontier exceeds the 8 concurrent generation slots split into two waves, generation requires 7 waves of one-segment requests, serial scoring adds 0.54 s, and the final survivors fit in 1 test wave. Applying the same duration and price arithmetic gives 19.92 s and $0.5447.

Systems insight: The beam generates 43.75 percent fewer output tokens, worth $0.054, but resupplying the task prompt and retained prefixes to every extension costs $0.272 more in input, so its token bill is the higher one. The knob is the price of repeated input. The beam’s bill falls below flat sampling’s only when input tokens cost less than 17.3 percent of list price, the regime a prefix cache creates. Its shorter generation waves already give it the lower timing subtotal under this schedule, before omitted prefill is added back. Quality does not appear in these totals; the choice between the two rests on the check results each produces.

3.6.5 Reserving budget before admitting work

Checking how many tokens a search has spent only after concurrent model calls return is too late to prevent a budget overrun, because eight in-flight calls can all finish and bill their full output allowance before the budget account updates. Before dispatching any candidate extension, the runtime must reserve the call’s maximum allowed output tokens along with its scorer and sandbox allowances, keep settled spend plus open reservations below the search ceilings, and release unused capacity only after the call settles. A post-call usage receipt tells the runtime what to settle, but only a pre-dispatch reservation can bound concurrent spend in advance.

↪ Charging every pending operation its worst-case ceiling until settlement is the bounded-liability admission developed in Reservation and settlement.

Search depth and wall-clock time also need separate limits. A depth cap limits the chain of sequential extensions or tool turns, while a deadline caps user-perceived latency. When the deadline expires, the runtime stops admitting new work, drops late responses from the expired phase, and sends cancellation signals to in-flight calls. As Streaming and cancellation showed, however, sending a cancel request does not guarantee that the remote server stops immediately; a server that ignores cancellation can keep decoding and billing up to the call’s full output ceiling. Until the remote service settles the request, the runtime must keep the full reservation charged against the budget.

Running out of budget ends the search without lowering the acceptance bar. A finished candidate can still fail regression tests, and a high learned score cannot stand in for a required check pass. If no candidate meets the acceptance contract when a ceiling is reached, the runtime reports an incomplete result, abstains, or escalates to a human operator. That separation is invariant closure (principle 1) applied to deliberation: mechanical limits sit below the model to stop spending, and acceptance evidence sits above the model to authorize returning an answer.

3.6.6 When to stop searching early

Hard ceilings stop a search when its allowance runs out, whereas early-stopping rules end the search inside the ceilings as soon as a candidate passes or the next round is no longer worth its cost. Many repair workloads need only the first candidate that meets their acceptance criteria, a stopping rule known as satisficing.9 Once a candidate passes the required checks and policy rules, the search stops and returns it. If the search stage checks only compilation, stopping at the first compiling patch hands testing and deployment authorization to the next stage of the pipeline.

9 Satisficing: Herbert Simon borrowed the word in 1956 to describe a chooser who stops at the first alternative meeting an aspiration level, because a decision maker with limited capacity cannot afford an exhaustive search for the best (Simon 1956). A runtime’s acceptance criteria play the role of that aspiration level, which is why they must be fixed before search begins rather than lowered as the budget runs out.

Simon, Herbert A. 1956. “Rational Choice and the Structure of the Environment.” Psychological Review 63 (2): 129–38. https://doi.org/10.1037/h0042769.

An explicit secondary objective can justify searching further after finding an acceptable candidate, such as looking for a smaller diff or a faster query plan within the remaining budget. A rising learned score by itself does not justify continuing if held-out benchmarks show that higher scores past the pass threshold rarely improve real outcomes. Continuing therefore requires an explicit estimate of what the next unit of work is worth.

Marginal stopping10 asks whether the expected acceptance gain from the next round is worth its added cost. During a live repair, the runtime does not know the exact probability that the next attempt will pass, so it estimates that gain from calibration curves measured on held-out tasks or from progress signals such as fewer failing tests. Because any single diagnostic is only a partial signal, those estimates hold only for workloads and test suites similar to the calibration set.

10 Sequential Stopping: Statistics made stopping part of an experiment’s design with the sequential probability ratio test, which after each observation decides whether to accept, reject, or sample again (Wald 1945). Such tests reach a verdict with fewer observations on average than a fixed-size test with the same error rates. The marginal rule borrows that structure, although its threshold is an economic choice rather than an error rate.

Wald, Abraham. 1945. “Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses.” The Annals of Mathematical Statistics 16 (2): 117–86. https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177731118.

For a nested sequence of round limits under the same workload and acceptance criterion, let \(\CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount}\) estimate the probability of finding an acceptable result by round \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\). Let \(\CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount}\) denote the expected cumulative expenditure of the policy that stops at its first acceptable result and otherwise runs up to that round limit. Before admitting round \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\), with no earlier acceptance, the marginal policy stops when

\[ \frac{\CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount} - \CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}}{\CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount} - \CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}} < \MarginalStoppingThreshold, \tag{7}\]

where \(\MarginalStoppingThreshold\) is the minimum required gain in acceptance probability per unit of spend. Because round \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\) executes only when the first \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount - 1\) rounds fail to produce an acceptable result (an event with probability \(1 - \CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}\)), the denominator \(\CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount} - \CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}\) equals the full cost of running round \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\) multiplied by \(1 - \CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}\). Dividing both numerator and denominator by that same reach probability \(1 - \CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}\) converts the numerator into the conditional probability of passing in round \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\) given earlier failure and cancels the reach probability from the denominator. The ratio in equation 7 therefore equals the conditional next-round pass probability divided by the full cost of running that round. Dividing the unconditional probability gain by the full round cost would mix two different denominators. In an adaptive search, these conditional estimates should be updated from the current search state rather than read blindly from an unconditional table.

The numerator measures the gain in the estimated chance of a verified pass, not the number of new candidates or a rise in a heuristic score. The verification asymmetry (principle 3) explains why, since generating more candidates and obtaining a verified solution are distinct events. A search whose calibrated chance of passing stays flat is burning budget without making progress, no matter how many tokens it emits. The threshold \(\MarginalStoppingThreshold\) is an economic choice set by the value of an automated fix relative to human escalation, net of checker error and delay; high failure costs never justify searching longer against an untrustworthy check.

For satisficing workloads, the runtime applies these three stopping checks in a fixed order before admitting any new work (figure 8). First, hard ceilings on tokens, calls, depth, or wall-clock time stop new admissions unconditionally; if an acceptable candidate is already in hand it goes to the acceptance gate, and otherwise the runtime abstains or escalates. Second, within the ceilings, any candidate that meets the acceptance criteria stops the search and goes to the acceptance gate. Third, when the ceilings still have room and no candidate has passed yet, the marginal stopping rule (equation 7) stops the search early if the next round’s expected gain per dollar falls below \(\MarginalStoppingThreshold\). Reversing this order would either let an optimistic gain estimate breach a hard ceiling or let a diminishing-returns check discard a candidate that already passed. Whichever condition ends the search, the runtime settles all open reservations and separates verified passes from unverified attempts.

Checkpoint 3.2: Accounting for a bounded search

The resource accounts and the stopping policy must count work even when it produces no accepted result.

Search structures, resource accounts, and stopping rules define how a single deliberation policy runs inside its bounds. Choosing among competing policies requires comparing their end-to-end pass rates, actual spend, and deadline completions on the same workload, charging every failed and timed-out attempt to the accepted results.

3.7 Evaluating Deliberation Policies

Suppose an iterative search policy resolves more connection-pool failures than a single-call baseline. That gain might come from the search algorithm itself, or it might come from a larger token budget, a stronger base model, or silently dropping timed-out tasks from the denominator. Judging any policy that combines candidate allocation, selection, verification checks, and early stopping therefore requires recording three things: the exact resource ceilings and permissions granted to each policy, the tokens, checks, and wall-clock time actually spent, and the fate of every task that failed to produce an accepted result.

3.7.1 Controlled and system-level comparisons

Comparing a multi-round search on a newer model against a single call on an older model, or counting only the tasks that finished before the deadline, mixes model capability and survivor bias with the effect of the search policy. A controlled policy comparison isolates the search policy by holding the base model, serving setup, benchmark tasks, tool interfaces, and acceptance criteria fixed while varying only the deliberation rule under the same resource ceilings and deadline. Prompt tokens, output tokens, check runs, and concurrency slots should each be stated as separate ceilings rather than folded into one vague “compute budget.” Because a good policy stops early when a candidate passes, unspent budget reflects efficiency rather than an unfair test.

↪ Complete agent policies are compared at matched budgets, with every initiated trial charged, by the method developed in Matched-budget comparison.

A system-design comparison answers a broader engineering question: how two complete stacks trade cost, latency, and accuracy against one another. For instance, an engineer might compare one call to a large model against repeated calls to a smaller, faster model under the same dollar and latency caps. That comparison tells the team which stack to deploy, but it cannot separate the effect of search from the difference between the two models. Keeping controlled policy tests separate from stack-level comparisons prevents teams from crediting a model upgrade to their search algorithm.

Evaluation logs must record every started task, including explicit failures, abstentions, and deadline timeouts. To avoid crediting a search for exploiting its own scorer (the Goodhart failure of section 3.4.3), final output quality must be graded by an independent check rather than the internal score that guided the search. Logs should also separate three failure modes: false acceptance (returning a broken candidate), abstention or escalation, and deadline timeout. When reporting checker error rates, state the denominator explicitly.11 The share of broken candidates that pass a check (\(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\)) differs from the share of passing candidates that are broken (\(1-\VerifierPrecision\)). Furthermore, because sampling and branching are stochastic, benchmarks need repeated runs across the task set rather than a single point number.

11 Two Error Denominators: The fraction of incorrect candidates that pass is the check’s false positive rate, \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) in this chapter; the fraction of accepted outputs that are incorrect is the false discovery rate, \(1-\VerifierPrecision\). Equation 3 connects them only through the valid-candidate rate, so a report that gives one without the base rate cannot be converted into the other.

Total spend includes every resource used along the way: pruned branches, repeated prompt prefill across turns, verifier runs, and tokens decoded in flight after a cancellation signal. Dividing the total spend across the whole task cohort by the number of tasks that produced an accepted result gives the effective cost per accepted task, the cohort measure of \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2). Because a slow repair that misses the service deadline is unusable, the share of tasks that finish before the deadline belongs right beside \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\); a low average latency can hide a long tail where sequential revisions time out.

3.7.2 Policies under a deadline

A deliberation policy that allows three feedback rounds on paper can run at most two rounds in practice when each round takes 30 seconds and the service deadline is 75 seconds. To see how concurrency limits and wall-clock deadlines constrain a policy in production, the notebook below compares three policies evaluated by a scoped test suite rather than an omniscient oracle. All probabilities are stipulated parameters chosen to show how concurrency, stopping rules, and token budgets interact under one deadline.

Napkin Math 3.7: Three policies, one deadline
Scenario: Consider an automated software repair workload checked by a sealed regression suite. An initial candidate passes the suite with hypothetical probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) = 40 percent, and repeated attempts on the same task draw candidates independently. Passing the suite is the acceptance rule for this stage, though passing tests does not guarantee that the patch is defect-free. Each policy runs under the same permissions and resource ceilings: at most 32,768 input tokens, 8,192 generated tokens, and 8 test runs, with up to 8 parallel workers and a hard 75 s deadline.

Each initial request sends 2,048 prompt tokens, each candidate generates 1,000 tokens, and running the test suite takes 10 s. Using the illustrative 21.075 ms per-token decode time from Estimating per-call hardware work, assume that concurrent streams keep the same per-token rate and that tests run on dedicated workers without contention (omitting prefill, queueing, network transfer, and filtering overhead). Token prices are $3 per million prompt tokens and $15 per million output tokens, with no prefix-cache discount.

Option A (single candidate): Generate and test one candidate. It passes the suite with probability 40 percent, spending 2,048 prompt tokens, 1,000 generated tokens, and one test run. The attempt finishes in 31.07 s at a model bill of $0.02114, and if the test fails the runtime abstains.

Option B (parallel breadth): Dispatch all 8 candidates at once without early stopping, test each as it finishes, and return the first passing patch in index order. At least one of the \(\CandidatePoolSize\) = 8 candidates passes with probability pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize = 1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^{\CandidatePoolSize}\) = 98.32 percent, which is also the chance of returning an accepted repair. Because all eight streams and tests fit within the concurrency limit, wall-clock time stays 31.07 s, while the fan-out spends 16,384 prompt tokens, 8,000 generated tokens, and 8 test runs for a model bill of $0.16915.

Option C (sequential feedback): Generate one candidate and stop right away if it passes. If it fails, append the failed patch and a 340-token failure trace to the prompt and request a revision, which passes with conditional probability \(\RevisionPassProbability\) = 35 percent. Although the policy nominally allows 3 rounds, three sequential rounds would take 93.22 s and miss the 75 s deadline. Only 2 complete rounds fit inside 75 s, so the deadline caps the policy at one revision and forces an abstention if the second attempt also fails.

Math: With the third round cut off by the deadline, sequential feedback passes with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability+(1-\AttemptPassProbability)\,\RevisionPassProbability\) = 61 percent and runs an expected \(1+(1-\AttemptPassProbability)\) = 1.6 rounds. Expected spend is 4,080.8 prompt tokens, 1,600 generated tokens, and 1.6 test runs, for an expected model bill of $0.03624 and an expected latency of 49.72 s (at most 62.15 s). The remaining 39 percent of tasks end without an accepted patch and must be counted as failed attempts. Across all started tasks, expected generated tokens per accepted repair are about 2,500 for Option A, 8,137 for Option B, and 2,623 for Option C.

Systems insight: Per accepted repair, the model bill is $0.0529 for Option A, $0.0594 for Option C, and $0.1720 for Option B (3.3× the single-candidate baseline). Sequential feedback lifts the pass rate from 40 percent to 61 percent at a small cost premium per accepted task, whereas parallel breadth reaches 98.32 percent at a much steeper bill per accepted repair.

The deadline decides which policy can run. When the service has room for only one 31.07 s round, parallel breadth is the only option here that improves on the 40 percent single-call pass rate. When 75 s allows two rounds, sequential feedback raises the pass rate at a much smaller token bill than breadth, while a third round at 93.22 s would never finish in time. In production, test-runner cost and false acceptance rates belong alongside the token bill so that passing the suite tracks real task fixes.

3.7.3 Choosing an operating point

No single policy in the comparison above wins on every axis: the single call is cheapest, parallel breadth finishes in one round at the highest pass rate, and two-round feedback reaches an intermediate pass rate at a much lower token bill than breadth. Each of the three sits on the Pareto frontier. A policy is strictly dominated only when another feasible policy matches or beats it on every metric (latency, token bill, checking load, and pass rate) and strictly beats it on at least one. Among non-dominated policies, the service’s deadline, budget cap, and error tolerance pick the operating point.

Picking that operating point requires testing under the same limits the service will enforce in production. Analytical formulas cannot plug an ad-hoc “effective pool size” into pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) when worker queues, prefix caching, and check runtimes reshape the actual cost and latency. Likewise, the gain from a feedback round depends on how informative the test failure trace actually is, and hard deadlines cut off multi-round searches before their planned final round. Benchmarks must therefore enforce the real worker timeouts, early-exit rules, and admission ceilings rather than extrapolating uncensored curves into a deadline-bound service.

3.8 Fallacies and Pitfalls

The most common mistakes in test-time compute come from treating extra work as proof of progress. Extra tokens, wider pools, and longer searches pay off only when the pool holds diverse hypotheses, the check separates valid repairs from plausible mistakes, and the budget account counts every discarded branch. When any of those pieces is missing, spending more compute only burns budget faster or selects more reliably for checker blind spots.

Fallacy: More generated tokens necessarily produce a better answer.

Extended reasoning can help a model trace constraints already present in the prompt, but it can just as easily elaborate and defend a wrong initial guess. When the open question concerns runtime behavior, generating tokens cannot supply the missing observation. In section 3.1, generating a 1,024-token explanation takes 21.58 s, 539.5× the 40 ms test run that directly separates the two hypotheses. Token count measures spend, not progress; extra reasoning tokens earn their keep only when benchmarks show they raise the accepted pass rate enough to justify their latency and cost.

Pitfall: Treating independent samples as independent capabilities.

Samples drawn independently for a given prompt still share the same model weights and blind spots, so plugging a workload’s average pass rate into pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) overstates what breadth can deliver (section 3.3.3). Suppose half the tasks in a workload pass at 60 percent per candidate and the other half never pass (\(\AttemptPassProbability = 0\)). The average single-sample pass rate across the workload is 30 percent, the same rate that notebook 3.3 used for a single task. Plugging that average into \(1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^{\CandidatePoolSize}\) predicts 94.2 percent success for a pool of 8, whereas the true success rate across the workload is only 50.0 percent because sampling never solves the half where \(\AttemptPassProbability = 0\). Always compute pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) per task first and average afterward.

Fallacy: A check with a low false acceptance rate makes almost every pass trustworthy.

How trustworthy a passing verdict is depends as much on the generator’s base rate of valid candidates as on the check’s error rates (equation 3). When valid candidates are scarce, even a small false acceptance rate lets broken candidates fill a large share of the passing pool. With a 2 percent false acceptance rate, a 10 percent false rejection rate, and a 5 percent base rate of valid candidates, only 70.31 percent of passing candidates are valid, and a first-passing selector returns a broken repair on 29.26 percent of task attempts (notebook 3.4). On hard tasks, raising the generator’s base rate matters just as much for precision as tightening the check.

Pitfall: Charging only for the final answer.

Pruned branches, repeated prompt prefill, test runs, and scorer calls all consume resources, and in-flight requests canceled at a deadline often keep billing until the remote server settles them. Pricing a deliberation policy by the tokens in its final returned answer hides most of its cost. In notebook 3.7, parallel breadth returns a single 1,000-token patch, but it bills 8,000 output tokens per task and spends 8,137 output tokens per passing task. Likewise, the beam search in notebook 3.6 reads 156,288 input tokens to deliver 4 final survivors. Production cost accounts must charge every discarded branch and failed attempt to the accepted tasks.

Fallacy: The highest-scoring candidate has completed the task.

A scorer orders candidates on whatever proxy it was built to measure, and picking the maximum score concentrates positive scoring errors into the winner. With equal true utilities and independent Gaussian noise, the winner among 64 candidates has an expected scoring error of about 2.3 standard deviations (equation 4), and a checker loophole present in only 0.5 percent of single candidates shows up in 27.4 percent of 64-candidate pools. A high relative score is neither a proof nor an authorization for properties the verifier did not test. When the budget runs out without a candidate passing the acceptance check, the top-scoring candidate is still an unverified failure.

3.9 Summary

Test-time compute turns the cost of a decision into an explicit runtime trade-off. While model weights fix what a single forward pass can do, the runtime chooses how much extra work to spend, whether to spend it on depth, breadth, or feedback, and what evidence justifies stopping. Extra deliberation lowers cost per accepted task only when three links all hold. Generation surfaces a valid candidate that a single call missed, the selector picks that candidate out of the pool, and the verifier checks the property the task actually requires. If any link fails, attempt cost rises while the acceptance rate \(\AcceptanceProbability\) stalls, driving up \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\). A deliberation policy should therefore be judged like any other systems component: by the accepted tasks it delivers per dollar and per second within the service deadline. Table 7 collects the equations derived in this chapter for pricing allocations, measuring pool availability and check precision, bounding search trees, and stopping early.

Key Takeaways: More work needs a bounded policy
  • Observe when the question is empirical: Generating more tokens re-examines what is already in the prompt, whereas running an external check brings in new runtime facts. In the connection-pool repair, generating one explanatory trace took 539.5× as long as the targeted probe that separated the two hypotheses.
  • A pool is only as good as its selector: Pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) caps what an unchanged-member selector can return, not what it actually returns. Averaging pass rates across a mixed workload naively predicts 94.2 percent success across 8 candidates when the true pool delivers only 50.0 percent, because extra samples cannot solve tasks where the model has zero chance of success.
  • A pass inherits the base rate: When valid candidates make up 5 percent of the generator’s output, a verifier with a 2 percent false acceptance rate leaves only 70.31 percent of passing candidates valid. Under the fixed IID first-passing rule, returned candidates have that same precision regardless of pool size. With equal true utilities and independent Gaussian noise, picking the top score among 64 candidates inflates the winner’s score by about 2.3 standard deviations of noise.
  • Revise along recorded dependencies: When an observation refutes an assumption, regenerate only the artifacts that depend on it rather than rebuilding the whole candidate plan. Missing dependency edges let stale conclusions survive silently, and plan revision never undoes side effects already committed to external systems.
  • Budgets end searches, not uncertainty: Every pruned branch, verifier call, and failed attempt belongs in the cost per accepted task \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), and service deadlines bound how many sequential rounds can run. When a budget or deadline runs out without an accepted candidate, the runtime must abstain or escalate rather than returning the top-scoring unverified candidate.

The verification asymmetry (principle 3) gives a practical order for diagnosing any deliberation policy whose cost per accepted task is too high. First check pool availability per task via pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\). If passing candidates rarely enter the pool, switch to a stronger model or escalate rather than sampling wider. If the pass rate for a task is zero under the current prompt and model, more samples will not help. Where availability is healthy, check \(\VerifierPrecision\) at the measured base rate, because a low precision means a better generator or a stricter check buys more than a larger pool. Only when both availability and precision are sound does the question become how many rounds of search to buy, which the marginal stopping rule answers in conditional acceptance gain per dollar. At every step, the runtime, not the model, decides whether to continue, abstain, or escalate.

3.10 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Spending compute at decision time goes back to the earliest game-playing programs. Claude Shannon’s 1950 design for a chess program separated generating candidate moves from scoring the resulting board positions with a heuristic evaluation function (Shannon 1950). Because game trees grow exponentially with depth, searching every branch to the end was impossible, and heuristic search, which generates candidates and prunes weak branches with domain evaluations, became a central theme of artificial intelligence (Newell and Simon 1976). Beam search entered through speech recognition in HARPY (Lowerre 1976), while the two stopping rules used in this chapter came from statistics and economics: Abraham Wald’s sequential probability ratio test (Wald 1945) and Herbert Simon’s satisficing (Simon 1956).

Shannon, Claude E. 1950. “Programming a Computer for Playing Chess.” The London, Edinburgh, and Dublin Philosophical Magazine and Journal of Science 41 (314): 256–75. https://doi.org/10.1080/14786445008521796.
Fikes, Richard E., Peter E. Hart, and Nils J. Nilsson. 1972. “Learning and Executing Generalized Robot Plans.” Artificial Intelligence 3: 251–88. https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(72)90051-3.
Doyle, Jon. 1979. “A Truth Maintenance System.” Artificial Intelligence 12 (3): 231–72. https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(79)90008-0.
Coulom, Rémi. 2006. “Efficient Selectivity and Backup Operators in Monte-Carlo Tree Search.” Proceedings of the 5th International Conference on Computers and Games (CG 2006), 72–83. https://doi.org/10.1007/11925231_6.
Silver, David, Aja Huang, Chris J. Maddison, Arthur Guez, Laurent Sifre, George van den Driessche, Julian Schrittwieser, et al. 2016. “Mastering the Game of Go with Deep Neural Networks and Tree Search.” Nature 529 (7587): 484–89. https://doi.org/10.1038/nature16961.

Dependency-directed revision has an equally long history in systems and planning. Early robotics planners such as STRIPS and PLANEX checked plan preconditions against sensor readings and replanned when an observation contradicted an assumed state (Fikes et al. 1972). Truth maintenance systems (Doyle 1979) and make (Feldman 1979) turned that idea into the explicit dependency graphs that revisable candidate plans use today. As search spaces grew wider, Monte Carlo tree search (Coulom 2006; Kocsis and Szepesv’ari 2006) introduced bandit-based tree expansion, and AlphaGo combined PUCT with neural policy and value networks to steer search through huge branching factors (Silver et al. 2016).

Language models brought these search mechanisms together over text and code. In program synthesis, pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) measured how much parallel sampling improves functional test passage (Kulal et al. 2019; Chen et al. 2021), and Cobbe et al. (2021) showed that training a separate verifier to rank sampled math solutions could beat scaling generator parameters alone. Chain-of-thought prompting extended depth within a single call (Wei et al. 2022), and self-consistency combined breadth with majority voting (Wang et al. 2023). Process supervision moved learned scoring from final answers to intermediate steps (Uesato et al. 2022; Lightman et al. 2024), which enabled Tree of Thoughts (Yao et al. 2023) to run beam and tree search over multi-step reasoning.

Wei, Jason, Xuezhi Wang, Dale Schuurmans, Maarten Bosma, Brian Ichter, Fei Xia, Ed H. Chi, Quoc V. Le, and Denny Zhou. 2022. “Chain-of-Thought Prompting Elicits Reasoning in Large Language Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 24824–37. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1800.
Wang, Xuezhi, Jason Wei, Dale Schuurmans, Quoc Le, Ed Chi, Sharan Narang, Aakanksha Chowdhery, and Denny Zhou. 2023. “Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models.” The Eleventh International Conference on Learning Representations.
Uesato, Jonathan, Nate Kushman, Ramana Kumar, Francis Song, Noah Siegel, Lisa Wang, Antonia Creswell, Geoffrey Irving, and Irina Higgins. 2022. “Solving Math Word Problems with Process- and Outcome-Based Feedback.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2211.14275, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2211.14275.
Yao, Shunyu, Dian Yu, Jeffrey Zhao, Izhak Shafran, Thomas L. Griffiths, Yuan Cao, and Karthik Narasimhan. 2023. “Tree of Thoughts: Deliberate Problem Solving with Large Language Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).
DeepSeek-AI, Daya Guo, Dejian Yang, et al. 2025. “DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2501.12948, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2501.12948.

More recent work treats test-time compute as an explicit budget to allocate across axes. Snell et al. (2024) showed that the best mix of sequential revision, parallel sampling, and verifier-guided search depends on task difficulty. Easy tasks favor revision, moderate tasks favor hybrid search, and tasks where the base model has near-zero success need a stronger model rather than more samples. Reinforcement learning on verifiable tasks then trained models to run long internal scratchpads that test ideas and backtrack inside a single generation (DeepSeek-AI et al. 2025). Finally, pushing search hard against learned reward models exposed Goodhart’s law (Goodhart 1984) in the form of reward-model overoptimization (Gao et al. 2023), where the winner’s proxy score keeps climbing while held-out accuracy peaks and falls.

  • Search as problem solving (Newell and Simon 1976). Introduces heuristic search as generating and pruning hypotheses under resource limits, the framework that test-time compute policies build on.
  • Monte Carlo tree search (Browne et al. 2012). Surveys tree-search selection rules, rollouts, and backup operators adapted for multi-step language-model search.
  • Trained verifiers (Cobbe et al. 2021). Shows that sampling candidates and ranking them with a trained verifier can beat scaling generator parameters alone.
  • Process supervision (Lightman et al. 2024). Compares outcome-level and step-level supervision, detailing the labeling cost and pruning benefits of process reward models.
  • Reward-model overoptimization (Gao et al. 2023). Measures how proxy reward scores diverge from held-out accuracy as best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) pool size grows.
  • Compute-optimal test-time allocation (Snell et al. 2024). Analyzes how the frontier across parallel sampling, revision, and tree search shifts with task difficulty under a fixed compute budget.
Newell, Allen, and Herbert A. Simon. 1976. “Computer Science as Empirical Inquiry: Symbols and Search.” Communications of the ACM 19 (3): 113–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/360018.360022.
Browne, Cameron B., Edward Powley, Daniel Whitehouse, Simon M. Lucas, Peter I. Cowling, Philipp Rohlfshagen, Stephen Tavener, Diego Perez, Spyridon Samothrakis, and Simon Colton. 2012. “A Survey of Monte Carlo Tree Search Methods.” IEEE Transactions on Computational Intelligence and AI in Games 4 (1): 1–43. https://doi.org/10.1109/TCIAIG.2012.2186810.
Cobbe, Karl, Vineet Kosaraju, Mohammad Bavarian, Mark Chen, Heewoo Jun, Lukasz Kaiser, Matthias Plappert, et al. 2021. “Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2110.14168.
Gao, Leo, John Schulman, and Jacob Hilton. 2023. “Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning 202: 10835–66.
Snell, Charlie, Jaehoon Lee, Kelvin Xu, and Aviral Kumar. 2024. “Scaling LLM Test-Time Compute Optimally Can Be More Effective Than Scaling Model Parameters.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2408.03314.

3.11 Exercises

Unless a problem states otherwise, use the hypothetical givens in table 8. They are problem inputs chosen for round arithmetic, not measurements of any model or service. Omit prefill, queueing, and transport time, and apply no prefix-cache discount unless a problem introduces one.

1. Three allocations, one repository (Basic). A repair request carries an \(8{,}000\)-token prompt and a ceiling of \(6{,}000\) generated tokens. Depth spends the whole ceiling on one candidate. Breadth generates six \(1{,}000\)-token candidates, but the service admits only three concurrent streams. Feedback runs \(\TurnHorizon = 3\) rounds of \(1{,}000\)-token candidates; each round ends with a 6 s check that returns a 500-token report, and later requests append every earlier candidate and report.

  1. Using equation 1, compute each allocation’s critical path, input tokens, output tokens, and model-token bill.
  2. The repository context grows to \(64{,}000\) tokens. Recompute the three bills and the breadth premium \((\CandidatePoolSize-1)\SequenceLength\) in tokens and dollars.
  3. The task owner now requires a result within 60 s. Which allocation meets the deadline, and at what bill? A prefix cache would charge re-read prompt tokens at 10 percent of list price. What does breadth then cost, and what omitted term in this accounting should an engineer measure before committing to it?

2. Sizing a pool against a target (Basic). For a fixed repair task, independent candidates pass the acceptance check with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability = 0.20\), and the selector returns a passing member whenever one exists.

  1. Find the smallest pool \(\CandidatePoolSize\) whose pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) reaches 90 percent, and the availability gained by its last candidate.
  2. Compare candidates spent per accepted repair for the parallel pool and for sequential retry that stops at the first pass and gives up after \(\CandidatePoolSize\) attempts. What does the parallel pool buy for its premium?
  3. The workload is actually mixed: half the tasks pass at \(0.40\) per candidate and half never pass, so the average rate is still \(0.20\). What pool success does the pool size \(\CandidatePoolSize\) from part (a) deliver, and what is the largest pool success any \(\CandidatePoolSize\) can deliver? What should the runtime do with the second half of the workload instead?
  4. In a benchmark, 4 of 20 samples for one task pass. Compute the subset estimate of pass@5 and compare it with the plug-in value \(1-(1-\PassingSampleCount/\GeneratedSampleCount)^5\).

3. How strict must the check be? (Basic). A candidate pool has valid-candidate rate \(\ValidCandidateRate = 0.10\). The current check has \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 0.05\) and \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.01\) and costs $0.10 per run.

  1. Compute the probability of a pass \(\VerifierPassProbability\) and the precision \(\VerifierPrecision\) from equation 3.
  2. Precision must reach 95 percent. Find the largest \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) that meets the target at this \(\ValidCandidateRate\), and alternatively the smallest \(\ValidCandidateRate\) that meets it with the current check.
  3. The policy pre-generates 32 candidates and checks them in a fixed order until the first pass, abstaining if none passes. Compute the probability of any selection, the probability that the batch contains at least one false pass, and the probability of selecting a defective candidate. Explain why the second and third differ.
  4. A stricter check has \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.005\) and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 0.08\) and costs $0.40 per run. A defective selection costs $2,000 in incident loss. Compare the two checks on expected incident loss plus expected checking cost per attempt, and decide which to deploy.

4. What step scoring costs (Intermediate). A learned process scorer reads a \(3{,}000\)-token task input and the growing prefix of a candidate built in \(\ScoredStepCount = 20\) steps of 150 tokens each. The runtime scores 64 candidate trajectories per task at the scorer price in table 8.

  1. Using equation 2, compute scorer input per trajectory and per task when every prefix is rescored from scratch, and when the interface supports incremental prefix reuse.
  2. For this \(\SequenceLength\) and \(\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\), beyond how many steps does the quadratic term exceed the \(\ScoredStepCount \SequenceLength\) term?
  3. Without prefix reuse, a team proposes scoring only every fourth step. Compute its scorer input and bill per task. A defective step is then caught on average 1.5 steps later, and every pruned trajectory generates those extra steps at the output price. Bound the added generation cost per task, and recommend a scoring stride with and without prefix reuse.

5. Keeping a tree finite (Intermediate). Each retained node proposes \(\SearchBranchingFactor = 4\) children of 200 generated tokens, a step check rejects each child independently with probability \(\ChildPruningProbability = 0.60\), and the search runs \(\SearchDepth = 5\) steps deep under a ceiling of \(20{,}000\) generated tokens.

  1. Compute \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor\) from equation 6 and the expected generated nodes and tokens with and without pruning. Which expected counts fall below the ceiling, and why does an expected count below it not guarantee that every run fits?
  2. What rejection rate would make the tree die out on its own, and why is a rejection rate that high rarely available?
  3. The step check also rejects valid steps with probability \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\). A correct solution needs five consecutive valid steps to survive. Compute its survival probability at \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 0.10\), and the largest \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\) that keeps survival at or above 80 percent. What does this imply for tuning the check’s threshold?

6. A waiting customer sets the policy (Intermediate). The support agent answers a policy question while the customer waits. Each request carries a \(6{,}000\)-token prompt of policy excerpts and conversation, each candidate reply is 150 output tokens, and a grounding check against the cited policy takes 1.5 s. A first reply passes with probability \(0.70\); conditional on a failed first reply, a revision that reads the check’s report passes with probability \(0.50\). Concurrent candidates are checked concurrently, and the reply must arrive within 8 s.

  1. Compute the duration of one generate-and-check round. How many feedback rounds fit the deadline, and what pass probability does feedback therefore deliver?
  2. For concurrent breadth of \(\CandidatePoolSize = 1\) to \(4\), compute pass probability, model-token bill per request, and bill per passing reply. Find the smallest \(\CandidatePoolSize\) that reaches 95 percent.
  3. The service can hold 600 concurrent decode streams for all sessions. How many simultaneous sessions does the choice in (b) support, compared with a single candidate? Which resource binds this policy?
  4. Contrast with the repair scenario (notebook 3.7), where a second feedback round fit the deadline. Name the constraint that changed the answer, and state one design change that would make feedback viable for the support agent.

7. Scoping a revision (Intermediate). A repair candidate records generated artifacts. Each is listed here with its output tokens and direct prerequisites: diagnosis \(\text{Dx}\) (300; source excerpt, failure observation), pool patch \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\) (600; source excerpt, \(\text{Dx}\)), metrics patch \(\RepairPatchArtifact_2\) (200; \(\text{Dx}\)), check selection \(\text{Chk}\) (150; \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\), test inventory), API notes \(\text{API}\) (250; API declaration), release note \(\text{Rel}\) (200; \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\), \(\RepairPatchArtifact_2\), \(\text{API}\)), and migration script \(\text{Mig}\) (400; \(\text{API}\)). The labels are local names for this problem only.

  1. A waiter-and-return test contradicts \(\text{Dx}\). List \(\text{Desc}(\text{Dx})\), and compute the output tokens of scoped regeneration (the diagnosis and its descendants) against full regeneration.
  2. Instead, the API declaration changes while the diagnosis stands. Which artifacts need review, and what share of the output does scoped regeneration avoid?
  3. The pool patch in fact calls the pool’s public constructor, so \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\) depends on the API declaration, but that edge was never recorded. After the declaration changes, which stale artifacts survive the traversal, and what could the runtime then wrongly reuse? Propose how the runtime should record edges so that this failure cannot occur silently, and state what that costs.

8. A judge under selection pressure (Advanced). The research agent drafts \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidate summaries of what it has read, and a learned judge scores each as \(\EstimatedCandidateUtility_i = \CandidateUtility_i + \CandidateScoringError_i\). True quality is \(\CandidateUtility_i \sim \GaussianDistribution(\CandidateUtilityMean, \CandidateUtilityStandardDeviation^2)\) with \(\CandidateUtilityStandardDeviation = 1.0\), scoring error is \(\CandidateScoringError_i \sim \GaussianDistribution(0, \ScoringErrorStandardDeviation^2)\) with \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation = 0.5\), all independent. The exact expected maximum of \(\CandidatePoolSize\) standard normal draws is 1.03, 1.77, and 2.34 for \(\CandidatePoolSize\) = 4, 16, and 64. Each candidate costs $0.12.

  1. Using the exact values in place of equation 4, bound the winner’s expected scoring error at each \(\CandidatePoolSize\).
  2. Show that when selection is on \(\EstimatedCandidateUtility\), the winner’s expected quality gain over \(\CandidateUtilityMean\) is \((\CandidateUtilityStandardDeviation^2/\EstimatedUtilityStandardDeviation)\,\ExpectedNormalMaximum\) and its expected error is \((\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation^2/\EstimatedUtilityStandardDeviation)\,\ExpectedNormalMaximum\), where \(\EstimatedUtilityStandardDeviation = \sqrt{\CandidateUtilityStandardDeviation^2 + \ScoringErrorStandardDeviation^2}\) and \(\ExpectedNormalMaximum\) is the expected standard-normal maximum. Evaluate both at the three pool sizes, and compare quality gained per dollar in moving from 4 to 16 and from 16 to 64 candidates.
  3. A fraction \(\ExploitCandidateFraction = 0.002\) of summaries cite a retrieved page whose planted text the judge rewards but which does not support the claim. Compute the probability that a pool of 4, 16, and 64 contains at least one such summary, and explain why selection then tends to return it.
  4. Redesign the selection policy. Choose a pool size and a check the planted page cannot influence, and state what evidence the returned summary must carry. How does the binding constraint here differ from the repair agent’s?

9. Stopping before an irreversible change (Advanced). The remediation agent generates candidate configuration changes in rounds, each costing $0.20 in generation and a staging check. Calibrated on held-out incidents, the estimated probability of a staging-accepted candidate after \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\) rounds is \(\CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount}\) = 0.40, 0.58, 0.68, 0.74, 0.77, 0.79 for \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\) = 1 to 6, with \(\CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_0 = 0\), and the hard ceiling is six rounds.

  1. For each prospective round, derive the conditional acceptance gain given no earlier pass and the increment in expected stopped-policy cost. Compute the marginal ratio of equation 7. With \(\MarginalStoppingThreshold = 0.25\) per dollar, how many rounds can the policy admit after earlier failures, and what are its acceptance probability and expected generation-and-check expenditure if it stops at the first accepted candidate?
  2. An accepted automated change saves $30 of on-call time compared with escalation. Derive \(\MarginalStoppingThreshold\) from that value, and state how many rounds the policy now admits and which rule ends the search.
  3. A colleague proposes that when the ceiling is reached without an accepted candidate, the agent applies its highest-scoring candidate. That candidate is defective with probability 0.5, a defective change costs $40,000, and escalating to a human costs $150. Compute each fallback’s expected loss conditional on reaching the ceiling without an accepted candidate, then its contribution per initiated task under the policy in (a). Treat deliberation expenditure as common to both alternatives. Identify the stopping-precedence rule the proposal violates.
  4. Write the redesigned policy as the ordered checks the runtime applies before admitting another round, including what it returns in each case. Why does a staging-accepted candidate still not authorize the production change?

What’s Next: From deliberation to working state
Deliberation leaves candidate versions, failed hypotheses, observations, and dependency records. The next model call cannot treat all of them as equally current or useful, even when every one of them still fits in its window. It needs a working context that retains the evidence supporting the selected candidate, distinguishes superseded material, and carries unresolved questions forward. Context Engineering develops this logical state-selection problem. Only after deciding what belongs in the working context does the physical question arise of how to store and reuse its model state, developed in KV Cache Management.
